and all integers $K\geq0$ (the definition of Schwartz data used in
\cite[Definition~1.1]{Tao2013}).  Indeed, if (4) holds then
$\sup_x(1+|x|)^K|\partial^\alpha u_0(x)|\leq C_{\alpha K}<\infty$ for every
integer $K\geq0$; conversely, if $u_0\in\mathcal S$ then for every $\alpha$
and every real $K\geq0$, with $\lceil K\rceil$ the least integer $\geq K$,
$|\partial^\alpha u_0(x)|\leq(1+|x|)^{-\lceil K\rceil}
\sup_y(1+|y|)^{\lceil K\rceil}|\partial^\alpha u_0(y)|
\leq C_{\alpha K}(1+|x|)^{-K}$ with
$C_{\alpha K}:=\sup_y(1+|y|)^{\lceil K\rceil}|\partial^\alpha u_0(y)|<\infty$,
because $(1+|x|)^{-\lceil K\rceil}\leq(1+|x|)^{-K}$.  Let $u$, $p$ be the
maximal classical solution and its normalised pressure on $[0,T_*)$ from
Proposition~\ref{prop:localtheory}.

\emph{Step 1: $T_*=\infty$.}  Suppose $T_*<\infty$.  Choose $H=T_*+1$, so
$\min\{H,T_*\}=T_*$.  Hypothesis~\ref{hyp:critical} gives
$\sup_{0<t<T_*}\norm{u(t)}_3\leq M(\nu,u_0,H)<\infty$, which is
\eqref{eq:endpoint}; Theorem~\ref{thm:continuation} then yields $T_*=\infty$,
a contradiction.  Hence $T_*=\infty$.

\emph{Step 2: smoothness on $\R^3\times[0,\infty)$ (Fefferman's (6)).}  Since
$T_*=\infty$, Lemma~\ref{lem:global-smooth} of the local-theory section (the
global form of (R2)) gives $u,p\in C^\infty(\R^3\times[0,\infty))$ in the
sense of \cite[p.~58, (6)]{Fefferman2000}: all partial derivatives
$\partial_t^j\partial_x^\alpha u$, $\partial_t^j\partial_x^\alpha p$ exist,
one-sided in $t$ at $t=0$, and are continuous on $\R^3\times[0,\infty)$.
(Explicitly: for $(x,t)\in\R^3\times[0,\infty)$ pick any $T>t$; since
$T<T_*=\infty$, (R2) gives $u,p\in C^\infty([0,T]\times\R^3)$, and the
derivatives computed on $[0,T]\times\R^3$ and on $[0,T']\times\R^3$, $T<T'$,
are derivatives of one and the same function.)

\emph{Step 3: the equations (1), (2), (3) with $f\equiv0$.}  By (R2),
$\partial_tu+(u\cdot\nabla)u+\nabla p=\nu\Delta u$ and $\nabla\cdot u=0$ hold
pointwise on $[0,T]\times\R^3$ for every $T$, hence on $\R^3\times[0,\infty)$;
by (R3), $u(x,0)=u_0(x)$.  These are Fefferman's (1) (with $f_i\equiv0$), (2),
and (3).  Alternative (A) of \cite[p.~58]{Fefferman2000} fixes $f\equiv0$,
so his condition (5) on the force is vacuous.

\emph{Step 4: bounded energy (Fefferman's (7)).}  For every $t\geq0$ pick
$T>t$; Proposition~\ref{prop:energy} on $[0,T]$ gives
$\int_{\R^3}|u(x,t)|^2\,dx\leq\norm{u_0}_2^2$.  Hence
$\int_{\R^3}|u(x,t)|^2\,dx<C$ for all $t\geq0$ with the single constant
$C=\norm{u_0}_2^2+1$, and $\sup_{t\geq0}\int|u(x,t)|^2dx<\infty$.

\emph{Step 5: the pressure.}  The pressure in Steps 2--3 is
$p=R_iR_j(u_iu_j)$, which by the convention paragraph equals
$-\Delta^{-1}\partial_i\partial_j(u_iu_j)$, Tao's normalised pressure; it is
the pressure of (R2) and is smooth on $\R^3\times[0,\infty)$ by Step~2.
Fefferman's statement requires some smooth $p$; this one qualifies, and no
freedom ``up to a function of time'' remains to be fixed.

Steps 2--5 verify (1), (2), (3), (6), (7) of \cite[pp.~57--58]{Fefferman2000}
for the data class (4) with $f\equiv0$, which is Theorem~\ref{def:target}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The maximal-$L^3$ route of Gallagher, Koch, and Planchon]
\label{rem:gkp}
Theorem~4 of \cite{GKP2013} (arXiv:1012.0145, \S``Serrin's endpoint
regularity criterion''; \emph{Math.\ Ann.}\ 355 (2013), \S3.1; unit viscosity)
states, verbatim: ``For any $u_0\in L^3(\R^3)$,
$\sup_{t\in[0,T^*(u_0))}\norm{NS(u_0)(t)}_{L^3(\R^3)}<\infty\Longrightarrow T^*(u_0)=+\infty$,''
where $NS(u_0)$ is the strong (Duhamel) solution with datum $u_0$ in the
class $E_{p,q}(T)$ and $T^*(u_0)$ its maximal time; the remark following it
confirms that the left side is $NS(u_0)\in L^\infty((0,T^*);L^3)$,
``or in the notation of [ESS], $NS(u_0)\in L_{3,\infty}(0,T^*)$.''  With
$a=\nu^{-1}u_0$ this corroborates the \emph{statement} of
Theorem~\ref{thm:continuation} for the maximal $L^3$ branch $NS(a)$: the
same kind of $L^3$ bound forces that branch to be global.  We do not use it,
because turning the corroboration into a proof for the classical branch $v$
of Lemma~\ref{lem:nu-normalisation} requires three further statements that
Theorem~4 does not contain: membership of $v$ in $E_{p,q}(T)$ together with
the Leray-projected Duhamel identity; a uniqueness theorem identifying $v$
with $NS(a)$ on every $[0,T]$, $T<S_*$; and, in the case $T^*(a)>S_*$, a
regularity theorem for $NS(a)$ beyond $S_*$ contradicting (R4).  The
uniqueness theorem usually invoked for the second point (Furioli,
Lemari\'e-Rieusset, and Terraneo, \emph{Rev.\ Mat.\ Iberoam.}\ 16 (2000)
605--667) was not inspected in its primary text.  The route through
Lemma~\ref{lem:leray-hopf} avoids all three: its hypotheses are checked line
by line against the printed definition of the Leray--Hopf class, and the
passage from $L^5(Q_{S_*})$ back to the classical branch is the
manuscript-owned Lemma~\ref{lem:serrin-enstrophy}, the case $s=l=5$ of the
Ladyzhenskaya--Prodi--Serrin condition $\frac3s+\frac2l=1$ of
\cite[Theorem~1.2, p.~213]{ESS2003}, proved here directly because an explicit
$H^1$ bound for the classical branch is needed rather than a smoothness
statement for weak solutions.  Theorem~\ref{thm:continuation} concerns only
the branch selected by Proposition~\ref{prop:localtheory}.  No converse from
an arbitrary smooth finite-energy solution class to the selected strong
branch is asserted here.
\end{remark}

\section{Compactness does not supply the bound}\label{sec:compactness}

A hypothetical singularity can be rescaled about points of large amplitude.
At critical scaling the $L^3$ norm is unchanged, so a uniform critical bound
can provide weak compactness and, with local energy and pressure estimates, a
suitable ancient limit.  The construction still requires normalization to
prevent a zero limit, control of translations and dilations, strong local
convergence sufficient for the nonlinear term, and passage of the local
energy inequality.  Profile-decomposition methods make these losses of
compactness explicit \cite{GKP2013}.

Even after those steps, compactness gives a candidate critical element or
ancient solution.  A separate rigidity theorem must exclude every such
nonzero limit.  Neither the required uniform bound nor that unrestricted
rigidity theorem follows from Propositions~\ref{prop:energy},
\ref{prop:scaling}, and \ref{prop:enstrophy}.  Thus compactness is a
conditional reduction, not a proof of Hypothesis~\ref{hyp:critical}.

\section{The cubic gradient quotient}
\label{sec:quotient}

A separate route avoids the explicit pressure work of
Section~\ref{sec:pressure}.  It rests on a functional on
$L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ whose derivative annihilates $\mathcal G_3$, the closed
space of $L^3$ gradients of Definition~\ref{def:quotient}(a) below.  This
section proves an unweighted regularity estimate for the minimizing
representative and records an analytic mechanism built on it; it proves no
estimate for the strain term that mechanism leaves open.  Its first part,
Definition~\ref{def:quotient} through
Proposition~\ref{prop:quotient-derivative}, concerns the functional alone:
it uses no property of Navier--Stokes solutions, and every statement says
whether it holds for all of $L^3$ or requires a divergence-free field
(Remark~\ref{rem:quotient-scope}).  No estimate for the remaining strain
term is proved anywhere in this section.

\begin{remark}[Related work]\label{rem:quotient-related}
The variational mechanism used here is not new.  Minimizing a convex
function of the pointwise norm over a coset of exact forms, and obtaining a
unique representative whose \emph{nonlinear} expression is coclosed, is
nonlinear Hodge theory: Sibner and Sibner proved exactly this for a closed
datum and for densities bounded above and below
\cite[\S3.2 and \S4]{SibnerSibner1970}, and the $L^p$ theory of
differential forms and the nonlinear Hodge systems built on it extend the
framework to $p$-growth densities on cohomology classes
\cite{Scott1995,IwaniecScottStroffolini1999}.  Those theorems are stated
for closed forms, whereas the datum below is a divergence-free field whose
curl is prescribed and in general nonzero; none of them is used as a step
in this section.  Likewise, $L^p$ Lyapunov functionals for Navier--Stokes
solutions under critical smallness, and the coercivity of the unshifted
heat generator $-\langle|\phi|^{p-2}\phi,\Delta\phi\rangle$ on which they
rest, are due to Kato \cite{Kato1990}, reproduced as Lemmas~2.5 and~2.6 of
\cite{MannaSritharan2007}; the case $p=3$ in three dimensions is the
unshifted counterpart of the quantities appearing below.  A bounded
literature search did not locate the shifted $L^3$ gradient quotient of
Definition~\ref{def:quotient} or its pressure-free evolution; that is a
statement about the reach of the search and not a novelty claim, and no
priority is claimed for anything in this section.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Conventions and imported facts}
\label{subsec:quotient-conventions}

All functions and fields in this section are real-valued unless a complex
Fourier transform is displayed.  For $z\in\R^3$, $|z|$ is the Euclidean
norm.  $L^r$ stands for $L^r(\R^3)$ or $L^r(\R^3;\R^3)$ with
$\norm f_r=(\int_{\R^3}|f|^r\,dx)^{1/r}$, and
$\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\R^3}f\cdot g\,dx$ whenever $f\cdot g\in L^1$; by
H\"older's inequality this is the case for $f\in L^{3/2}$, $g\in L^3$,
with $|\langle f,g\rangle|\le\norm f_{3/2}\norm g_3$.  Fix
$\chi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ with $0\le\chi\le1$, $\chi=1$ on
$\{|x|\le1\}$, $\chi=0$ on $\{|x|\ge2\}$, and set $\chi_R(x)=\chi(x/R)$
for $R\ge1$, so that $\norm{\nabla\chi_R}_\infty=R^{-1}\norm{\nabla\chi}_\infty$
and $\norm{\nabla\chi_R}_6=R^{-1/2}\norm{\nabla\chi}_6$.  Fix a mollifier
$\rho\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ with $\rho\ge0$, $\int\rho=1$,
$\operatorname{supp}\rho\subset\{|x|\le1\}$, and set
$\rho_\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon^{-3}\rho(x/\varepsilon)$.  A field
$u\in L^1_{\rm loc}(\R^3;\R^3)$ is \emph{solenoidal} if
$\int_{\R^3}u\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx=0$ for every $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$,
that is, if $\operatorname{div}u=0$ in the sense of distributions.  The
Fourier transform is $\hat f(\xi)=\int_{\R^3}e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}f(x)\,dx$
for $f\in L^1(\R^3;\mathbb C)$, taken componentwise for fields; this is
the convention of \cite{Tao2013}, under which the Riesz transforms of
Section~\ref{sec:pressure} have symbols $-i\xi_j/|\xi|$.
$\mathcal S(\R^3)$ is the Schwartz space.

The following facts are imported; each is stated in the form in which it
is used, with the source in which it was checked.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(F1)] \emph{Reflexivity and weak sequential compactness.}  For a
 $\sigma$-finite measure space and $1<p<\infty$, $L^p$ is reflexive
 \cite[Thm.~4.10, p.~95]{Brezis2011}; every bounded sequence in a reflexive
 Banach space has a weakly convergent subsequence
 \cite[Thm.~3.18, pp.~69--70]{Brezis2011}.
\item[(F2)] \emph{Weak closedness and lower semicontinuity.}  In a Banach
 space a convex set is weakly closed if and only if it is norm closed
 \cite[Thm.~3.7, p.~60]{Brezis2011}; if $x_n\rightharpoonup x$ weakly then
 $\norm x\le\liminf_n\norm{x_n}$ \cite[Prop.~3.5(iii), p.~58]{Brezis2011}.
\item[(F3)] \emph{Convolution.}  For $k\in L^1(\R^3)$ and
 $g\in L^p(\R^3)$, $1\le p\le\infty$, the integral
 $(k*g)(x)=\int_{\R^3}k(x-y)g(y)\,dy$ converges absolutely for a.e.\ $x$
 and $\norm{k*g}_p\le\norm k_1\norm g_p$ \cite[Thm.~4.15, p.~104]{Brezis2011}.
 For $k\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ and $g\in L^1_{\rm loc}(\R^3)$, $k*g\in
 C^\infty(\R^3)$ with $\partial^\alpha(k*g)=(\partial^\alpha k)*g$
 \cite[Prop.~4.20, p.~107]{Brezis2011}.  For $k\in L^1(\R^3)$ and $g\in L^p(\R^3)$,
 $1\le p\le\infty$, one has
 $\operatorname{supp}(k*g)\subset\operatorname{supp}k+\operatorname{supp}g$
 \cite[Prop.~4.18, p.~106]{Brezis2011}; in particular $k*g$ has compact
 support when $k$ and $g$ both do.  For $g\in L^p(\R^3)$, $1\le p<\infty$,
 $\rho_\varepsilon*g\to g$ in $L^p$ as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$
 \cite[Thm.~4.22, p.~109]{Brezis2011}.
\item[(F4)] \emph{Plancherel.}  The map $f\mapsto\hat f$ on
 $L^1\cap L^2(\R^3;\mathbb C)$ extends uniquely to a unitary operator
 $\mathcal F$ of $L^2(\R^3;\mathbb C)$, whose inverse extends
 $g\mapsto\check g$, $\check g(x)=\int_{\R^3}e^{2\pi ix\cdot\xi}g(\xi)\,d\xi$,
 from $L^1\cap L^2$; and $\int f\,\overline g\,dx=\int\mathcal Ff\,
 \overline{\mathcal Fg}\,d\xi$ for $f,g\in L^2$
 \cite[\S2.2.4, p.~113]{Grafakos2014}, the section ``The Fourier
 Transform on $L^1+L^2$''; the transform convention there is the one fixed
 above, so this citation imports no factor of $2\pi$.  For fields,
 $\mathcal F$ acts componentwise.
\item[(F5)] \emph{Leray projection on $L^p$.}  Let $\mathbb P$ be the
 operator on $L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(a) below, the
 orthogonal projection onto the solenoidal subspace.  For every
 $1<p<\infty$ there is $C_p<\infty$ such that
 $\norm{\mathbb Pf}_p\le C_p\norm f_p$ for all
 $f\in L^2\cap L^p(\R^3;\R^3)$.  This is stated on page~38 of
 \cite{Tao2013}: ``If $u$ is square-integrable, then $\mathbb Pu$ is the
 orthogonal projection of $u$ onto the space of square-integrable
 divergence-free vector fields; from Calder\'on--Zygmund theory, we know
 that the projection $\mathbb P$ is bounded on $L^p_x(\R^3)$ for every
 $1<p<\infty$.''  The underlying theorem is the $L^p$ boundedness of the
 Riesz transforms \cite[Ch.~II, \S2; Ch.~III, \S1]{Stein1970}, since
 $(\mathbb Pf)_i=f_i+R_iR_jf_j$ (summation over $j$) has the symbol
 $\delta_{ij}-\xi_i\xi_j|\xi|^{-2}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(a).  Only $p=3$, $p=\frac32$, $p=6$ and $p=9$ are used, and we write
$C_{\mathbb P}$, $C_{3/2}$, $C_6$, $C_9$ for the corresponding operator
norms, so that $C_6,C_9<\infty$ by the same Calder\'on--Zygmund statement
quoted above from page~38 of \cite{Tao2013} and
\cite[Ch.~II, \S2; Ch.~III, \S1]{Stein1970}.  For each such $p$ the
operator $\mathbb P$ extends uniquely from $L^2\cap L^p$ to a bounded
operator on $L^p(\R^3;\R^3)$ of norm at most $C_p$: this is the argument
of Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(b) with $3$ replaced by $p$, the truncations
$f_n=f\mathbf1_{\{|x|\le n\}}\mathbf1_{\{|f|\le n\}}$ of
Lemma~\ref{lem:density}(b) lying in $L^2\cap L^p$ and converging to $f$ in
$L^p$ by dominated convergence with dominant $(2|f|)^p$.  These extensions
are consistent: if $f\in L^p\cap L^r$ with $1<p,r<\infty$, the same
sequence $(f_n)$ converges to $f$ in both norms, so $\mathbb Pf_n$
converges to the two extensions in $L^p$ and in $L^r$ respectively, and a
subsequence converging a.e.\ shows the two limits agree a.e.  In
particular, for a solenoidal $u\in H^1$ with $w=w(u)\in L^3\cap L^6$, the
identity $\mathbb Pw=u$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-coercive} gives
$\norm u_6\le C_6\norm w_6$, and if moreover $w\in L^9$ it gives
$\norm u_9\le C_9\norm w_9$.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Definition and pointwise inequalities}

\begin{definition}[Gradient space, cubic quotient, auxiliary operators]
\label{def:quotient}\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $\mathcal G_3$ is the closure in $L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ of the set
 $\{\nabla\phi:\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)\}$.  This set is a linear
 subspace, and the closure of a linear subspace of a normed space is a
 linear subspace (limits of sums and multiples are sums and multiples of
 limits); hence $\mathcal G_3$ is a closed linear subspace of
 $L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$.
\item[(b)] For $v\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ put
 $F(v)=\frac13\int_{\R^3}|v|^3\,dx=\frac13\norm v_3^3$ and $j(v)=|v|v$.
 For $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ define
 \[
  \mathcal Q(u)=\inf_{q\in\mathcal G_3}F(u+q)
  =\inf_{q\in\mathcal G_3}\frac13\norm{u+q}_3^3 .
 \]
\item[(c)] For $\lambda>0$ and $a\in\R^3$, $(\mathcal D_\lambda u)(x)=\lambda u(\lambda x)$
 and $(\tau_au)(x)=u(x-a)$.
\item[(d)] For $s>0$ let $k_s(x)=(4\pi s)^{-3/2}e^{-|x|^2/4s}$ and
 $G_sf=k_s*f$; this is the heat semigroup $e^{s\Delta}$ in the explicit
 form of \cite[p.~39]{Tao2013}.  One has $k_s\in\mathcal S(\R^3)$, because
 every derivative $\partial^\alpha k_s$ is a polynomial in $x$ times
 $e^{-|x|^2/4s}$ and therefore decays faster than any power of $|x|$;
 $k_s>0$, and $\int_{\R^3}k_s\,dx=1$ because
 $\int_{\R}e^{-t^2/4s}\,dt=(4\pi s)^{1/2}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Pointwise cubic inequalities]\label{lem:cubic-pointwise}
Let $f(z)=\frac13|z|^3$ and $j(z)=|z|z$ for $z\in\R^3$.  Then
$f\in C^1(\R^3)$ with $\nabla f=j$, and for all $a,b,h\in\R^3$:
\begin{align}
 |j(a)-j(b)|&\le(|a|+|b|)\,|a-b|,
 \label{eq:cp-lipschitz}\\
 \bigl|f(a+h)-f(a)-j(a)\cdot h\bigr|&\le(|a|+|h|)\,|h|^2,
 \label{eq:cp-taylor}\\
 \bigl(j(a)-j(b)\bigr)\cdot(a-b)
 &=(|a|+|b|)\Bigl(\tfrac12(|a|-|b|)^2+\tfrac12|a-b|^2\Bigr)
 \ \ge\ \tfrac12|a-b|^3,
 \label{eq:cp-monotone}\\
 f\!\left(\tfrac{a+b}2\right)&\le\tfrac12\bigl(f(a)+f(b)\bigr),
 \quad\text{with equality only if }a=b.
 \label{eq:cp-strict}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $f(z)=\frac13(|z|^2)^{3/2}$ and $t\mapsto t^{3/2}$ is $C^1$ on
$[0,\infty)$ with derivative $\frac32t^{1/2}$, the chain rule gives
$f\in C^1(\R^3)$ and $\nabla f(z)=\frac13\cdot\frac32|z|\cdot2z=|z|z=j(z)$.

\eqref{eq:cp-lipschitz}: $j(a)-j(b)=|a|(a-b)+(|a|-|b|)b$ and
$\bigl||a|-|b|\bigr|\le|a-b|$.

\eqref{eq:cp-taylor}: $g(\theta)=f(a+\theta h)$ is $C^1$ on $[0,1]$ with
$g'(\theta)=j(a+\theta h)\cdot h$, so
\[
 f(a+h)-f(a)-j(a)\cdot h=\int_0^1\bigl(j(a+\theta h)-j(a)\bigr)\cdot h\,d\theta .
\]
By \eqref{eq:cp-lipschitz},
$|(j(a+\theta h)-j(a))\cdot h|\le(|a+\theta h|+|a|)\,\theta|h|\,|h|
\le(2|a|+|h|)\,\theta|h|^2$, and $\int_0^1\theta\,d\theta=\frac12$.

\eqref{eq:cp-monotone}: expanding,
$(|a|a-|b|b)\cdot(a-b)=|a|^3+|b|^3-(|a|+|b|)\,a\cdot b$.  Insert
$|a|^3+|b|^3=(|a|+|b|)(|a|^2-|a||b|+|b|^2)$ and
$a\cdot b=\frac12(|a|^2+|b|^2-|a-b|^2)$:
\[
 (|a|a-|b|b)\cdot(a-b)
 =(|a|+|b|)\Bigl(\tfrac12|a|^2+\tfrac12|b|^2-|a||b|+\tfrac12|a-b|^2\Bigr),
\]
which is the stated identity.  The inequality follows from
$|a|+|b|\ge|a-b|$.

\eqref{eq:cp-strict}: for $s,t\ge0$,
$\frac12(s^3+t^3)-\bigl(\frac{s+t}2\bigr)^3=\frac38(s+t)(s-t)^2\ge0$.
With $s=|a|$, $t=|b|$ and $|a+b|\le|a|+|b|$,
\[
 \Bigl|\frac{a+b}2\Bigr|^3\le\Bigl(\frac{|a|+|b|}2\Bigr)^3
 \le\frac12\bigl(|a|^3+|b|^3\bigr).
\]
Suppose equality holds throughout and $a\ne b$.  Equality in the second
inequality forces $(|a|+|b|)(|a|-|b|)^2=0$; since $a\ne b$ excludes
$a=b=0$, this gives $|a|=|b|=:r>0$.  Equality in the first gives
$|a+b|=2r$, so $4r^2=|a+b|^2=2r^2+2a\cdot b$, that is, $a\cdot b=r^2=|a||b|$,
and the equality case of the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality with $|a|=|b|$
gives $a=b$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The cubic functional on $L^3$]\label{lem:cubic-frechet}
Let $v,v',h\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$.  Then $j(v)\in L^{3/2}(\R^3;\R^3)$ with
$\norm{j(v)}_{3/2}=\norm v_3^2$, and
\begin{gather}
 \bigl|F(v+h)-F(v)-\langle j(v),h\rangle\bigr|
 \le(\norm v_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2,
 \label{eq:cp-F-taylor}\\
 \norm{j(v)-j(v')}_{3/2}\le(\norm v_3+\norm{v'}_3)\norm{v-v'}_3,
 \label{eq:cp-F-lipschitz}\\
 \langle j(v)-j(v'),v-v'\rangle\ge\tfrac12\norm{v-v'}_3^3 .
 \label{eq:cp-F-monotone}
\end{gather}
In particular $F$ is continuous on $L^3$, and $h\mapsto\langle j(v),h\rangle$
is a bounded linear functional on $L^3$ of norm at most $\norm v_3^2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$\int|j(v)|^{3/2}=\int|v|^3$ gives the first claim.  For
\eqref{eq:cp-F-taylor}, apply \eqref{eq:cp-taylor} pointwise with
$a=v(x)$, $h=h(x)$, integrate, and use H\"older's inequality with exponents
$3$ and $3/2$:
$\int(|v|+|h|)|h|^2\le\norm{|v|+|h|}_3\,\norm{|h|^2}_{3/2}
\le(\norm v_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2$.  For \eqref{eq:cp-F-lipschitz},
raise \eqref{eq:cp-lipschitz} to the power $3/2$, integrate, and apply the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality:
\[
 \int|j(v)-j(v')|^{3/2}
 \le\int(|v|+|v'|)^{3/2}|v-v'|^{3/2}
 \le\Bigl(\int(|v|+|v'|)^3\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\int|v-v'|^3\Bigr)^{1/2},
\]
so $\norm{j(v)-j(v')}_{3/2}\le\norm{|v|+|v'|}_3\norm{v-v'}_3
\le(\norm v_3+\norm{v'}_3)\norm{v-v'}_3$.  \eqref{eq:cp-F-monotone} is
\eqref{eq:cp-monotone} integrated; the left side is finite because
$j(v)-j(v')\in L^{3/2}$ and $v-v'\in L^3$.  Continuity of $F$:
$|F(v+h)-F(v)|\le\norm v_3^2\norm h_3+(\norm v_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2$ by
\eqref{eq:cp-F-taylor} and H\"older.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two density facts]\label{lem:density}\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] If $f\in L^1_{\rm loc}(\R^3;\mathbb C)$ and
 $\int_{\R^3}f\eta\,dx=0$ for every real-valued $\eta\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$,
 then $f=0$ a.e.  The same holds componentwise for fields.
\item[(b)] For $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ put
 $u_n=u\,\mathbf 1_{\{|x|\le n\}}\mathbf 1_{\{|u|\le n\}}$.  Then
 $u_n\in L^2\cap L^3\cap L^\infty$, $|u_n|\le|u|$, and $u_n\to u$ in
 $L^3$.  In particular $L^2\cap L^3$ is dense in $L^3$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Taking real and imaginary parts, assume $f$ real.  For fixed $x$ and
$\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, $\eta=\rho_\varepsilon(x-\cdot)$ is an admissible
test function, so $(\rho_\varepsilon*f)(x)=0$ for every $x$.  Fix $r>0$
and put $f_r=f\,\mathbf 1_{\{|y|\le r+1\}}\in L^1(\R^3)$.  For
$|x|\le r$ the integrand $\rho_\varepsilon(x-y)f(y)$ vanishes unless
$|y|\le r+1$, so $(\rho_\varepsilon*f_r)(x)=(\rho_\varepsilon*f)(x)=0$.
By (F3), $\rho_\varepsilon*f_r\to f_r$ in $L^1(\R^3)$, hence $f=f_r=0$
a.e.\ on $\{|x|\le r\}$; $r$ is arbitrary.

(b) $u_n$ is bounded by $n$ and supported in $\{|x|\le n\}$, so
$u_n\in L^2\cap L^\infty$; $|u_n|\le|u|$ gives $u_n\in L^3$; and
$u_n(x)\to u(x)$ for a.e.\ $x$ (for every $x$ at which $u(x)$ is finite),
so $\norm{u_n-u}_3\to0$ by dominated convergence with dominant $(2|u|)^3$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The minimizing representative}

\begin{lemma}[Existence, uniqueness, and Euler--Lagrange condition]
\label{lem:quotient-minimizer}
Let $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] There is $q\in\mathcal G_3$ with $F(u+q)=\mathcal Q(u)$.
\item[(b)] This $q$ is unique.  Write $q(u)$ for it, $w(u)=u+q(u)$ and
 $A(u)=j(w(u))=|w(u)|w(u)$.  Then $A(u)\in L^{3/2}$,
 $\mathcal Q(u)=\frac13\norm{w(u)}_3^3$ and $\norm{A(u)}_{3/2}=\norm{w(u)}_3^2$.
\item[(c)] $\langle A(u),g\rangle=0$ for every $g\in\mathcal G_3$.  In
 particular $\int_{\R^3}|w|w\cdot\nabla\phi\,dx=0$ for every
 $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$, that is, $\operatorname{div}(|w|w)=0$ in the
 sense of distributions, where $w=w(u)$.
\item[(d)] For every $g\in\mathcal G_3$: $\mathcal Q(u+g)=\mathcal Q(u)$,
 $w(u+g)=w(u)$, and $q(u+g)=q(u)-g$.
\end{enumerate}
All four statements hold for every $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Put $I=\mathcal Q(u)$.  Since $q=0$ is admissible and $F\ge0$,
$0\le I\le F(u)<\infty$.  Choose $q_n\in\mathcal G_3$ with
$F(u+q_n)\to I$ and, discarding finitely many terms,
$F(u+q_n)\le F(u)+1$ for all $n$.  Then
\[
 \norm{q_n}_3\le\norm{u+q_n}_3+\norm u_3
 \le\bigl(3F(u)+3\bigr)^{1/3}+\norm u_3=:M
 \qquad\text{for all }n .
\]
Let $X$ be the space $L^3$ of the product of Lebesgue measure on $\R^3$
with counting measure on $\{1,2,3\}$, a $\sigma$-finite measure space;
as a set $X=L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$, with norm
$\norm v_X=(\sum_{i=1}^3\int|v_i|^3)^{1/3}$.  The norms are equivalent:
$\norm v_X^3\le3\norm v_3^3$ because $|v_i|\le|v|$, and
$\norm v_3^3\le\int(|v_1|+|v_2|+|v_3|)^3\le9\norm v_X^3$ because
$(\frac{a_1+a_2+a_3}3)^3\le\frac13(a_1^3+a_2^3+a_3^3)$ for $a_i\ge0$ by
convexity of $t\mapsto t^3$ on $[0,\infty)$.  Hence
$(L^3(\R^3;\R^3),\norm\cdot_3)$ and $X$ have the same bounded sets, the
same continuous linear functionals, and therefore the same weakly
convergent sequences; completeness likewise transfers, so
$(L^3(\R^3;\R^3),\norm\cdot_3)$ is itself a Banach space, which is the
space in which (F2) is applied below.  By (F1), $X$ is reflexive and the
bounded sequence $(q_n)$ has a subsequence $(q_{n_k})$ converging weakly
to some $q\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$.  $\mathcal G_3$ is convex (a linear subspace) and
norm closed, hence weakly closed by (F2), so $q\in\mathcal G_3$.  For
every continuous linear functional $\ell$,
$\ell(u+q_{n_k})=\ell(u)+\ell(q_{n_k})\to\ell(u)+\ell(q)=\ell(u+q)$, so
$u+q_{n_k}\rightharpoonup u+q$ weakly, and by (F2)
$\norm{u+q}_3\le\liminf_k\norm{u+q_{n_k}}_3$.  Since $t\mapsto t^3/3$ is
increasing and continuous on $[0,\infty)$,
$F(u+q)\le\liminf_kF(u+q_{n_k})=I$.  As $q\in\mathcal G_3$, also
$F(u+q)\ge I$.  Thus $F(u+q)=I$.

(b) Let $q,q'\in\mathcal G_3$ both attain the infimum and put $w=u+q$,
$w'=u+q'$.  Then $\frac12(w+w')=u+\frac12(q+q')$ is admissible, so
$F(\frac12(w+w'))\ge I$.  By \eqref{eq:cp-strict} applied pointwise,
$f(\frac12(w+w'))\le\frac12(f(w)+f(w'))$ everywhere, with strict
inequality on the set $\{w\ne w'\}$.  If that set had positive measure,
integration would give $F(\frac12(w+w'))<\frac12(F(w)+F(w'))=I$, a
contradiction.  Hence $w=w'$ a.e., that is, $q=q'$.  The remaining claims
are Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-frechet} with $v=w(u)$ and the definition of
$\mathcal Q$.

(c) Let $g\in\mathcal G_3$, $w=w(u)$, $q=q(u)$, and put
$\varphi(s)=F(w+sg)$ for $s\in\R$.  Since $q+sg\in\mathcal G_3$, the field
$w+sg=u+(q+sg)$ is admissible, so $\varphi(s)\ge\varphi(0)$ for all $s$.
For a.e.\ $x$ the function $s\mapsto f(w(x)+sg(x))$ is $C^1$ with
derivative $j(w(x)+sg(x))\cdot g(x)$, and for $|s|\le1$
\[
 \bigl|j(w+sg)\cdot g\bigr|\le|w+sg|^2|g|\le(|w|+|g|)^2|g|,
 \qquad
 \int_{\R^3}(|w|+|g|)^2|g|\,dx\le\norm{|w|+|g|}_3^2\norm g_3<\infty
\]
by H\"older's inequality with exponents $3/2$ and $3$.  For
$0<|s|\le1$ the difference quotient
$s^{-1}\bigl(f(w+sg)-f(w)\bigr)$ is, by the mean value theorem, bounded
pointwise by the integrable function $(|w|+|g|)^2|g|$ and converges
pointwise to $j(w)\cdot g$ as $s\to0$.  Dominated convergence gives
$\varphi'(0)=\int j(w)\cdot g\,dx=\langle A(u),g\rangle$.  Since
$\varphi$ has a minimum at $s=0$, the one-sided limits of
$s^{-1}(\varphi(s)-\varphi(0))$ are $\ge0$ for $s\downarrow0$ and
$\le0$ for $s\uparrow0$; both equal $\varphi'(0)$, so
$\langle A(u),g\rangle=0$.  Taking $g=\nabla\phi$ with
$\phi\in C_c^\infty$ gives the distributional statement.

(d) Since $\mathcal G_3$ is a linear space,
$\{u+g+q':q'\in\mathcal G_3\}=\{u+q:q\in\mathcal G_3\}$; the two
minimization problems have the same admissible set and the same
functional, so the same value and, by (b), the same minimizer
$w(u+g)=w(u)$, whence $q(u+g)=w(u)-(u+g)=q(u)-g$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Reflexivity of $L^3$ is used only in Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer}(a).
Uniqueness uses only the pointwise strict convexity \eqref{eq:cp-strict};
no uniform convexity of $L^3$ and no Clarkson inequality is used in our
arguments.  (Brezis's proof of the imported reflexivity statement of (F1)
does use Clarkson's first inequality \cite[p.~95]{Brezis2011}; that is a
property of the import, not of the arguments given here.)  No regularity
of $w(u)$ beyond membership in $L^3$ is asserted; the representative
satisfies only the nonlinear divergence condition of (c).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Gradients, the Leray projection, and coercivity}

\begin{lemma}[Gradient closure]\label{lem:gradient-closure}
Let $\psi\in L^3(\R^3)$ have a distributional gradient
$\nabla\psi\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$.  Then $\nabla\psi\in\mathcal G_3$.  In
particular $\nabla\psi\in\mathcal G_3$ for every $\psi\in\mathcal S(\R^3)$
and every $\psi\in C^1(\R^3)$ with $\psi,\nabla\psi\in L^3$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Cutoff.}  Put $\psi_R=\chi_R\psi$.  For $\eta\in C_c^\infty$,
$\int\psi_R\,\partial_i\eta=\int\psi\,\partial_i(\chi_R\eta)
-\int\psi\eta\,\partial_i\chi_R
=-\int(\chi_R\partial_i\psi+\psi\,\partial_i\chi_R)\eta$,
so $\psi_R\in L^3$ has compact support and distributional gradient
$\nabla\psi_R=\chi_R\nabla\psi+\psi\nabla\chi_R\in L^3$, and
\[
 \norm{\nabla\psi_R-\nabla\psi}_3
 \le\norm{(1-\chi_R)\nabla\psi}_3+R^{-1}\norm{\nabla\chi}_\infty\norm\psi_3
 \longrightarrow0\qquad(R\to\infty)
\]
by dominated convergence in the first term.

\emph{Mollification.}  By (F3), $\rho_\varepsilon*\psi_R\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$
and $\partial_i(\rho_\varepsilon*\psi_R)=(\partial_i\rho_\varepsilon)*\psi_R$.
For fixed $x$, $y\mapsto\rho_\varepsilon(x-y)$ is in $C_c^\infty$, so by
the definition of the distributional gradient
\[
 (\partial_i\rho_\varepsilon)*\psi_R(x)
 =\int(\partial_i\rho_\varepsilon)(x-y)\psi_R(y)\,dy
 =-\int\partial_{y_i}\bigl[\rho_\varepsilon(x-y)\bigr]\psi_R(y)\,dy
 =\int\rho_\varepsilon(x-y)\,\partial_i\psi_R(y)\,dy ,
\]
that is, $\nabla(\rho_\varepsilon*\psi_R)=\rho_\varepsilon*\nabla\psi_R$,
which tends to $\nabla\psi_R$ in $L^3$ as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ by (F3).
Given $\delta>0$ choose $R$ with $\norm{\nabla\psi_R-\nabla\psi}_3<\delta$
and then $\varepsilon$ with
$\norm{\rho_\varepsilon*\nabla\psi_R-\nabla\psi_R}_3<\delta$; the
gradient of the $C_c^\infty$ function $\rho_\varepsilon*\psi_R$ is then
within $2\delta$ of $\nabla\psi$ in $L^3$.  Hence
$\nabla\psi\in\mathcal G_3$.  For $\psi\in\mathcal S$ or
$\psi\in C^1$ with $\psi,\nabla\psi\in L^3$, the classical gradient is the
distributional one (integration by parts against $\eta\in C_c^\infty$).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Leray projection]\label{lem:leray}\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] For $\xi\ne0$ let $\Pi(\xi)=I-\xi\otimes\xi/|\xi|^2$, a real
 symmetric $3\times3$ matrix with $\Pi(\xi)^2=\Pi(\xi)$ and
 $\Pi(\xi)\xi=0$.  For $f\in L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ define
 $\mathbb Pf=\mathcal F^{-1}\bigl(\Pi\,\mathcal Ff\bigr)$, which is
 meaningful because $\Pi$ is defined and bounded off the Lebesgue null set
 $\{0\}$, so $\Pi\,\mathcal Ff$ is defined a.e.  Then
 $\mathbb P$ is linear, $\norm{\mathbb Pf}_2\le\norm f_2$,
 $\mathbb P^2=\mathbb P$, $\langle\mathbb Pf,g\rangle=\langle f,\mathbb Pg\rangle$
 for $f,g\in L^2$, $\mathbb Pf$ is real-valued, and
 $\{f\in L^2:\mathbb Pf=f\}$ is exactly the set of solenoidal fields in
 $L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$.  Thus $\mathbb P$ is the orthogonal projection of
 $L^2(\R^3;\R^3)$ onto its solenoidal subspace, and $\mathbb Pu=u$ for
 every solenoidal $u\in L^2$.
\item[(b)] $\mathbb P$ extends uniquely from $L^2\cap L^3$ to a bounded
 linear operator on $L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$, again denoted $\mathbb P$, with
 $C_{\mathbb P}:=\norm{\mathbb P}_{L^3\to L^3}\le C_3<\infty$; on $L^3$
 one has $\mathbb P^2=\mathbb P$, and $\mathbb P$ maps real fields to
 real fields.  Moreover $\norm{\mathbb P\psi}_{3/2}\le C_{3/2}\norm\psi_{3/2}$
 for $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\R^3)$.
\item[(c)] $\mathbb P\nabla\phi=0$ for every $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$,
 and consequently $\mathbb Pq=0$ for every $q\in\mathcal G_3$.
\item[(d)] If $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ is solenoidal, then $\mathbb Pu=u$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) $\Pi(\xi)^2=I-2\xi\otimes\xi/|\xi|^2+\xi(\xi\cdot\xi)\xi/|\xi|^4=\Pi(\xi)$
and $\Pi(\xi)\xi=\xi-\xi(\xi\cdot\xi)/|\xi|^2=0$.  A real symmetric
idempotent matrix is an orthogonal projection, so $|\Pi(\xi)z|\le|z|$ for
$z\in\mathbb C^3$; hence $\Pi\,\mathcal Ff\in L^2$ with
$\norm{\Pi\mathcal Ff}_2\le\norm{\mathcal Ff}_2$, and by (F4)
$\mathbb Pf$ is well defined with $\norm{\mathbb Pf}_2\le\norm f_2$.
$\mathbb P^2=\mathbb P$ follows from $\Pi^2=\Pi$.  For $f,g\in L^2$ real,
(F4) and the symmetry of the real matrix $\Pi$ give
\[
 \langle\mathbb Pf,g\rangle
 =\int\Pi\mathcal Ff\cdot\overline{\mathcal Fg}\,d\xi
 =\int\mathcal Ff\cdot\overline{\Pi\mathcal Fg}\,d\xi
 =\langle f,\mathbb Pg\rangle .
\]
Realness: for real $f\in L^1\cap L^2$ the definition gives
$\hat f(-\xi)=\overline{\hat f(\xi)}$; the set of $g\in L^2(\R^3;\mathbb C^3)$
with $g(-\xi)=\overline{g(\xi)}$ a.e.\ is closed and $\mathcal F$ is
continuous, so $\mathcal Ff$ has this symmetry for every real
$f\in L^2$.  Since $\Pi(-\xi)=\Pi(\xi)$ is real, $\Pi\mathcal Ff$ has the
same symmetry.  For $g\in L^1\cap L^2$ with $g(-\xi)=\overline{g(\xi)}$,
$\overline{\check g(x)}=\int e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\overline{g(\xi)}\,d\xi
=\int e^{-2\pi ix\cdot\xi}g(-\xi)\,d\xi=\check g(x)$, so $\check g$ is
real; for general such $g\in L^2$ apply this to
$g\,\mathbf 1_{\{|\xi|\le n\}}$, which has the same symmetry, and pass to
the $L^2$ limit.  Hence $\mathbb Pf$ is real.

Range: $\mathbb Pf=f$ iff $\Pi\mathcal Ff=\mathcal Ff$ a.e.\ iff
$\xi\,(\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff)/|\xi|^2=0$ a.e.\ iff $\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff(\xi)=0$
for a.e.\ $\xi$.  We show that $f\in L^2$ is solenoidal iff
$\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff=0$ a.e.  For $\phi\in\mathcal S(\R^3)$ (real),
integration by parts in the Fourier integral gives
$\mathcal F(\partial_j\phi)=2\pi i\xi_j\mathcal F\phi$, so by (F4)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-sol-fourier}
 \langle f,\nabla\phi\rangle
 =\int\mathcal Ff\cdot\overline{2\pi i\xi\,\mathcal F\phi}\,d\xi
 =-2\pi i\int(\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff)\,\overline{\mathcal F\phi}\,d\xi .
\end{equation}
If $\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff=0$ a.e., the right side vanishes for every
$\phi\in C_c^\infty$, so $f$ is solenoidal.  Conversely let $f$ be
solenoidal.  First, $\langle f,\nabla\phi\rangle=0$ for every real
$\phi\in\mathcal S$: indeed $\chi_R\phi\in C_c^\infty$ and
$\nabla(\chi_R\phi)-\nabla\phi=(\chi_R-1)\nabla\phi+\phi\nabla\chi_R\to0$
in $L^2$ as $R\to\infty$ (dominated convergence, and
$\norm{\phi\nabla\chi_R}_2\le R^{-1}\norm{\nabla\chi}_\infty\norm\phi_2$).
Next, let $\eta\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\mathbb C)$ and $\phi=\check\eta$.
Differentiating under the integral sign and integrating by parts in $\xi$,
\[
 x^\beta\partial^\alpha\phi(x)
 =\Bigl(-\frac1{2\pi i}\Bigr)^{|\beta|}
 \int e^{2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\,\partial_\xi^\beta\bigl[(2\pi i\xi)^\alpha\eta(\xi)\bigr]\,d\xi
\]
is bounded for all multi-indices $\alpha,\beta$, so
$\phi\in\mathcal S(\R^3;\mathbb C)$, and $\mathcal F\phi=\eta$ by (F4).
Write $\phi=\phi_1+i\phi_2$ with $\phi_1,\phi_2$ real Schwartz functions;
then $\eta=\mathcal F\phi_1+i\mathcal F\phi_2$ and by
\eqref{eq:cp-sol-fourier} applied to $\phi_1$ and $\phi_2$,
\[
 \int(\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff)\,\overline\eta\,d\xi
 =\int(\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff)\,\overline{\mathcal F\phi_1}\,d\xi
 -i\int(\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff)\,\overline{\mathcal F\phi_2}\,d\xi
 =-\frac1{2\pi i}\bigl(\langle f,\nabla\phi_1\rangle-i\langle f,\nabla\phi_2\rangle\bigr)=0 .
\]
Since $\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff\in L^2_{\rm loc}\subset L^1_{\rm loc}$ and
$\eta$ ranges over all of $C_c^\infty$ (in particular over all real
$\eta$), Lemma~\ref{lem:density}(a) gives $\xi\cdot\mathcal Ff=0$ a.e.
So the fixed space of $\mathbb P$ is the solenoidal subspace, which is
therefore closed, and a self-adjoint idempotent with a given range is the
orthogonal projection onto it.

(b) By (F5) with $p=3$, $\norm{\mathbb Pf}_3\le C_3\norm f_3$ for
$f\in L^2\cap L^3$.  For $u\in L^3$ choose $u_n\in L^2\cap L^3$ with
$u_n\to u$ in $L^3$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:density}(b)); then $(\mathbb Pu_n)$
is Cauchy in $L^3$, its limit does not depend on the chosen sequence
(interlace two sequences), and setting $\mathbb Pu$ equal to this limit
defines a linear operator with $\norm{\mathbb Pu}_3\le C_3\norm u_3$
which agrees with (a) on $L^2\cap L^3$ (constant sequences).  Real fields
go to real fields (a.e.\ limits of real fields).  For
$u_n\in L^2\cap L^3$ one has $\mathbb Pu_n\in L^2\cap L^3$ and
$\mathbb P(\mathbb Pu_n)=\mathbb Pu_n$ by (a); letting $n\to\infty$ and
using continuity on $L^3$ gives $\mathbb P^2u=\mathbb Pu$.  The last
claim is (F5) with $p=3/2$, since $C_c^\infty\subset L^2\cap L^{3/2}$.

(c) For $\phi\in C_c^\infty$, $\mathcal F(\nabla\phi)=2\pi i\xi\,\hat\phi$,
so $\mathcal F(\mathbb P\nabla\phi)=2\pi i\,\Pi(\xi)\xi\,\hat\phi=0$ and
$\mathbb P\nabla\phi=0$.  For $q\in\mathcal G_3$ choose
$\phi_n\in C_c^\infty$ with $\nabla\phi_n\to q$ in $L^3$; since
$\nabla\phi_n\in L^2\cap L^3$, continuity of $\mathbb P$ on $L^3$ gives
$\mathbb Pq=\lim\mathbb P\nabla\phi_n=0$.

(d) Let $u\in L^3$ be solenoidal and fix $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\R^3)$.

\emph{Step 1 (duality).}  Let $u_n\in L^2\cap L^3$ with $u_n\to u$ in
$L^3$.  By (a), $\langle\mathbb Pu_n,\psi\rangle=\langle u_n,\mathbb P\psi\rangle$.
As $n\to\infty$ the left side tends to $\langle\mathbb Pu,\psi\rangle$
because $\mathbb Pu_n\to\mathbb Pu$ in $L^3$ and $\psi\in L^{3/2}$, and
the right side tends to $\langle u,\mathbb P\psi\rangle$ because
$\mathbb P\psi\in L^{3/2}$ by (b).  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-duality}
 \langle\mathbb Pu-u,\psi\rangle=-\langle u,\psi-\mathbb P\psi\rangle .
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 2 ($\psi-\mathbb P\psi$ is a gradient).}  Put
$\hat\psi=\mathcal F\psi$.  For every multi-index $\alpha$,
$(2\pi i\xi)^\alpha\hat\psi=\mathcal F(\partial^\alpha\psi)$ is bounded by
$\norm{\partial^\alpha\psi}_1$, so $|\hat\psi(\xi)|\le C_N(1+|\xi|)^{-N}$
for every $N$.  Define, for $\xi\ne0$,
\[
 g(\xi)=\frac{\xi\cdot\hat\psi(\xi)}{2\pi i\,|\xi|^2},
 \qquad |g(\xi)|\le\frac{|\hat\psi(\xi)|}{2\pi|\xi|} .
\]
Since $|\xi|^{-1}$ and $|\xi|^{-2}$ are integrable on $\{|\xi|\le1\}\subset\R^3$
and $\hat\psi$ decays rapidly, $g\in L^1\cap L^2$ and
$|\xi|^kg\in L^1\cap L^2$ for every $k\ge0$.  Put $\Theta=\check g$.
Differentiation under the integral sign, with dominant
$|2\pi\xi|^{|\alpha|}|g|\in L^1$, shows $\Theta\in C^\infty(\R^3)$ with
$\partial^\alpha\Theta=\bigl((2\pi i\xi)^\alpha g\bigr)^\vee$ bounded.
$\Theta$ is real: $\hat\psi(-\xi)=\overline{\hat\psi(\xi)}$ because $\psi$
is real, and $\overline{(2\pi i)^{-1}}=-(2\pi i)^{-1}$, so
$g(-\xi)=\overline{g(\xi)}$, and as in the proof of (a) this gives
$\overline{\Theta}=\Theta$.  By (F4), $\Theta\in L^2$ with $\mathcal F\Theta=g$
and $\partial_i\Theta\in L^2$ with
$\mathcal F(\partial_i\Theta)=2\pi i\xi_ig=\xi_i(\xi\cdot\hat\psi)/|\xi|^2$.
On the other hand,
$\mathcal F(\psi-\mathbb P\psi)=(I-\Pi)\hat\psi=\xi(\xi\cdot\hat\psi)/|\xi|^2$.
Since $\mathcal F$ is injective on $L^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-gradient-rep}
 \psi-\mathbb P\psi=\nabla\Theta\quad\text{a.e.},
 \qquad \Theta\in C^\infty(\R^3)\cap L^2(\R^3)\ \text{real},
 \qquad \nabla\Theta\in L^{3/2}(\R^3;\R^3),
\end{equation}
where the last membership is (b).

\emph{Step 3 (cutoff).}  Since $\chi_R\Theta\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ and $u$ is
solenoidal, $\langle u,\nabla(\chi_R\Theta)\rangle=0$ for every $R\ge1$.
Now $\nabla(\chi_R\Theta)-\nabla\Theta=(\chi_R-1)\nabla\Theta+\Theta\nabla\chi_R$,
and
\begin{align*}
 \norm{(\chi_R-1)\nabla\Theta}_{3/2}&\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{(dominated convergence, since }\nabla\Theta\in L^{3/2}),\\
 \norm{\Theta\nabla\chi_R}_{3/2}&\le\norm{\nabla\chi_R}_6\norm\Theta_2
 =R^{-1/2}\norm{\nabla\chi}_6\norm\Theta_2\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{(H\"older, }\tfrac23=\tfrac16+\tfrac12).
\end{align*}
Since
$u\in L^3$, $\langle u,\nabla\Theta\rangle=\lim_{R\to\infty}\langle u,\nabla(\chi_R\Theta)\rangle=0$.

\emph{Step 4.}  By \eqref{eq:cp-duality} and \eqref{eq:cp-gradient-rep},
$\langle\mathbb Pu-u,\psi\rangle=-\langle u,\nabla\Theta\rangle=0$ for
every $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3;\R^3)$.  Since $\mathbb Pu-u\in L^3\subset L^1_{\rm loc}$,
Lemma~\ref{lem:density}(a) gives $\mathbb Pu=u$ a.e.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The displayed formula ``$\mathbb Pu:=\Delta^{-1}(\nabla\times\nabla\times u)$''
on page~38 of \cite{Tao2013} differs by a sign from the operator defined
in Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(a) when $\Delta^{-1}$ is the multiplier
$-(4\pi^2|\xi|^2)^{-1}$ of his equation~(14), because
$\nabla\times\nabla\times u=\nabla(\nabla\cdot u)-\Delta u$; the operator
meant there is pinned by the accompanying sentence quoted in (F5), and the
$L^p$ bound is insensitive to the sign.  Only that sentence is imported.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Coercivity on solenoidal fields]\label{lem:quotient-coercive}
For every $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$, $\mathcal Q(u)\le F(u)=\frac13\norm u_3^3$.
If $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$ is solenoidal, then with $w=w(u)$ and
$q=q(u)$ one has $\mathbb Pw=u$, $q=w-\mathbb Pw$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-coercive}
 \frac{\norm u_3^3}{3C_{\mathbb P}^3}\le\mathcal Q(u)\le\frac13\norm u_3^3,
 \qquad
 \norm q_3\le(1+C_{\mathbb P})\norm w_3
 =(1+C_{\mathbb P})\bigl(3\mathcal Q(u)\bigr)^{1/3} .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The upper bound is the admissible choice $q=0$.  Let $u$ be solenoidal.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(d) and (c), $\mathbb Pw=\mathbb Pu+\mathbb Pq=u$,
hence $q=w-u=w-\mathbb Pw$ and
$\norm u_3=\norm{\mathbb Pw}_3\le C_{\mathbb P}\norm w_3$, that is,
$\norm u_3^3\le C_{\mathbb P}^3\norm w_3^3=3C_{\mathbb P}^3\mathcal Q(u)$.
Finally $\norm q_3\le\norm w_3+\norm{\mathbb Pw}_3\le(1+C_{\mathbb P})\norm w_3$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Homogeneity, scaling, and heat monotonicity}

\begin{lemma}[Homogeneity, critical scaling, translations]
\label{lem:quotient-scaling}
For every $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$, $\alpha\in\R$, $\lambda>0$ and $a\in\R^3$,
\[
 \mathcal Q(\alpha u)=|\alpha|^3\mathcal Q(u),\qquad
 \mathcal Q(\mathcal D_\lambda u)=\mathcal Q(u),\qquad
 \mathcal Q(\tau_au)=\mathcal Q(u),
\]
and $w(\alpha u)=\alpha w(u)$, $w(\mathcal D_\lambda u)=\mathcal D_\lambda w(u)$,
$w(\tau_au)=\tau_aw(u)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $\alpha=0$ both sides vanish since $q=0$ gives $F(0)=0\le\mathcal Q(0)$.
For $\alpha\ne0$, $q\mapsto\alpha q$ is a bijection of $\mathcal G_3$ and
$F(\alpha v)=|\alpha|^3F(v)$, so
$\mathcal Q(\alpha u)=\inf_{q}F(\alpha(u+q))=|\alpha|^3\mathcal Q(u)$;
the infimum is attained at $\alpha q(u)$, so $w(\alpha u)=\alpha w(u)$ by
uniqueness.  For the scaling, the change of variables $y=\lambda x$ gives
$\norm{\mathcal D_\lambda v}_3^3=\int\lambda^3|v(\lambda x)|^3dx=\norm v_3^3$,
so $\mathcal D_\lambda$ is a linear isometry of $L^3$ with inverse
$\mathcal D_{1/\lambda}$.  For $\phi\in C_c^\infty$,
$\mathcal D_\lambda\nabla\phi=\lambda(\nabla\phi)(\lambda\,\cdot)
=\nabla[\phi(\lambda\,\cdot)]$ with $\phi(\lambda\,\cdot)\in C_c^\infty$;
thus $\mathcal D_\lambda$ maps the generating set of $\mathcal G_3$ into
itself, and being an isometry it maps $\mathcal G_3$ into $\mathcal G_3$
(if $\nabla\phi_n\to q$ then $\mathcal D_\lambda\nabla\phi_n\to\mathcal D_\lambda q$,
and $\mathcal G_3$ is closed).  Applying this to $\mathcal D_{1/\lambda}$
shows $\mathcal D_\lambda\mathcal G_3=\mathcal G_3$.  Hence
\[
 \mathcal Q(\mathcal D_\lambda u)
 =\inf_{q\in\mathcal G_3}F(\mathcal D_\lambda u+q)
 =\inf_{q'\in\mathcal G_3}F(\mathcal D_\lambda(u+q'))
 =\inf_{q'\in\mathcal G_3}F(u+q')=\mathcal Q(u),
\]
attained at $q=\mathcal D_\lambda q(u)$, so
$w(\mathcal D_\lambda u)=\mathcal D_\lambda w(u)$.  Translations are
handled identically: $\tau_a$ is an isometry of $L^3$,
$\tau_a\nabla\phi=\nabla(\tau_a\phi)$, and $\tau_{-a}$ is the inverse.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Heat monotonicity]\label{lem:quotient-heat}
Let $s>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] For $f\in L^3$ the integral defining $G_sf(x)$ converges
 absolutely for a.e.\ $x$, and $\norm{G_sf}_3\le\norm f_3$.
\item[(b)] For $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$, $G_s\phi\in\mathcal S(\R^3)$ and
 $\nabla(G_s\phi)=G_s\nabla\phi$.
\item[(c)] $G_s\mathcal G_3\subset\mathcal G_3$.
\item[(d)] For every $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$, with $w=w(u)$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-heat}
  \mathcal Q(G_su)\le F(G_sw)\le F(w)=\mathcal Q(u).
 \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) This is the case $p=3$, $\norm{k_s}_1=1$, of Young's inequality (F3),
recorded on page~39 of \cite{Tao2013}; the constant $1$ is the point, so
we include the two-line proof.  By H\"older's inequality with exponents
$3/2$ and $3$ applied to $k_s^{2/3}\cdot k_s^{1/3}|f(x-\cdot)|$,
\[
 \int k_s(y)|f(x-y)|\,dy
 \le\Bigl(\int k_s\Bigr)^{2/3}\Bigl(\int k_s(y)|f(x-y)|^3dy\Bigr)^{1/3}
 =\bigl(k_s*|f|^3\bigr)(x)^{1/3}.
\]
By Tonelli's theorem $\int\!\!\int k_s(y)|f(x-y)|^3\,dy\,dx=\norm f_3^3<\infty$,
so $k_s*|f|^3<\infty$ a.e., the defining integral converges absolutely
a.e., and $\norm{G_sf}_3^3\le\int k_s*|f|^3\,dx=\norm f_3^3$.

(b) By (F3) with $k=\phi\in C_c^\infty$ and $g=k_s\in L^1_{\rm loc}$,
$G_s\phi=\phi*k_s\in C^\infty$ and
$\partial^\alpha(G_s\phi)=(\partial^\alpha\phi)*k_s=G_s\partial^\alpha\phi$.
Let $\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset\{|y|\le R_0\}$.  For $|x|\ge2R_0$
and $|y|\le R_0$ one has $|x-y|\ge|x|-R_0\ge|x|/2$, so
\[
 |\partial^\alpha(G_s\phi)(x)|
 \le\int_{|y|\le R_0}k_s(x-y)|\partial^\alpha\phi(y)|\,dy
 \le(4\pi s)^{-3/2}e^{-|x|^2/16s}\norm{\partial^\alpha\phi}_1 ,
\]
which decays faster than any power of $|x|$; together with boundedness on
$\{|x|\le2R_0\}$ this shows $x^\beta\partial^\alpha(G_s\phi)$ is bounded
for all $\alpha,\beta$, that is, $G_s\phi\in\mathcal S$.

(c) Let $q\in\mathcal G_3$ and $\phi_n\in C_c^\infty$ with
$\nabla\phi_n\to q$ in $L^3$.  By (b), $G_s\nabla\phi_n=\nabla(G_s\phi_n)$
with $G_s\phi_n\in\mathcal S$, so $G_s\nabla\phi_n\in\mathcal G_3$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:gradient-closure}.  By (a),
$\norm{G_s\nabla\phi_n-G_sq}_3\le\norm{\nabla\phi_n-q}_3\to0$, and
$\mathcal G_3$ is closed; hence $G_sq\in\mathcal G_3$.

(d) $G_sw=G_su+G_sq(u)$ with $G_sq(u)\in\mathcal G_3$ by (c), so $G_sw$
is admissible for $\mathcal Q(G_su)$ and $\mathcal Q(G_su)\le F(G_sw)$.
By (a), $F(G_sw)=\frac13\norm{G_sw}_3^3\le\frac13\norm w_3^3=F(w)=\mathcal Q(u)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-heat} holds for every $u\in L^3$ and every
$s>0$; it uses neither the semigroup property nor strong continuity of
$G_s$, and it does not differentiate or regularize the minimizer $w$.
Iterating it, $s\mapsto\mathcal Q(G_su)$ is nonincreasing, since
$G_{s+t}u=G_t(G_su)$ for $s,t>0$: the one-dimensional Gaussian integral
$\int_\R e^{-2\pi ix\xi}(4\pi s)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/4s}\,dx=e^{-4\pi^2s\xi^2}$
gives $\mathcal Fk_s(\xi)=e^{-4\pi^2s|\xi|^2}$, hence
$\mathcal F(k_s*k_t)=\mathcal Fk_s\,\mathcal Fk_t=\mathcal Fk_{s+t}$ by
Fubini's theorem, and $\mathcal F$ is injective on $L^2$ by (F4), so
$k_s*k_t=k_{s+t}$; associativity of convolution,
$k_t*(k_s*u)=(k_t*k_s)*u$, again by Fubini's theorem, then gives
$G_t(G_su)=G_{s+t}u$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The derivative}

\begin{lemma}[Stability of the minimizer]\label{lem:quotient-stability}
Let $u,h\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$, $w=w(u)$, $w'=w(u+h)$, $A=A(u)$, $A'=A(u+h)$.
Then
\begin{gather}
 \bigl|\norm{w'}_3-\norm w_3\bigr|\le\norm h_3,
 \label{eq:cp-contraction}\\
 \tfrac12\norm{w'-w}_3^3\le\langle A'-A,\,h\rangle,
 \qquad
 \norm{w'-w}_3\le2\,(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{1/2}\norm h_3^{1/2},
 \label{eq:cp-strong}\\
 \norm{A'-A}_{3/2}\le4\,(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{3/2}\norm h_3^{1/2},
 \qquad
 |\mathcal Q(u+h)-\mathcal Q(u)|\le(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^2\norm h_3 .
 \label{eq:cp-continuity}
\end{gather}
In particular $u\mapsto w(u)$ is continuous $L^3\to L^3$,
$u\mapsto A(u)$ is continuous $L^3\to L^{3/2}$, and $\mathcal Q$ is
continuous on $L^3$.  All of this holds for every $u\in L^3$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Quotient contraction.}  $w+h=(u+h)+q(u)$ is admissible for $u+h$,
so $\norm{w'}_3\le\norm{w+h}_3\le\norm w_3+\norm h_3$; symmetrically
$w'-h=u+q(u+h)$ is admissible for $u$, so
$\norm w_3\le\norm{w'}_3+\norm h_3$.  This is \eqref{eq:cp-contraction}.

\emph{Cancellation.}  $w'-w=h+\bigl(q(u+h)-q(u)\bigr)$ with
$q(u+h)-q(u)\in\mathcal G_3$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer}(c)
for the two minimizers gives $\langle A',w'-w\rangle=\langle A',h\rangle$
and $\langle A,w'-w\rangle=\langle A,h\rangle$, hence
$\langle A'-A,w'-w\rangle=\langle A'-A,h\rangle$.

\emph{Strong convergence.}  By \eqref{eq:cp-F-monotone} with $v=w'$,
$v'=w$, and the cancellation,
$\frac12\norm{w'-w}_3^3\le\langle A'-A,w'-w\rangle=\langle A'-A,h\rangle$,
the first part of \eqref{eq:cp-strong}.  By H\"older and
\eqref{eq:cp-F-lipschitz},
\[
 \langle A'-A,h\rangle\le\norm{A'-A}_{3/2}\norm h_3
 \le(\norm w_3+\norm{w'}_3)\norm{w'-w}_3\norm h_3 .
\]
If $w'\ne w$, dividing by $\norm{w'-w}_3$ and using
\eqref{eq:cp-contraction} gives
$\norm{w'-w}_3^2\le2(\norm w_3+\norm{w'}_3)\norm h_3
\le2(2\norm w_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3\le4(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3$,
which is the second part of \eqref{eq:cp-strong}; if $w'=w$ it is
trivial.

\emph{Continuity.}  By \eqref{eq:cp-F-lipschitz}, \eqref{eq:cp-contraction}
and \eqref{eq:cp-strong},
$\norm{A'-A}_{3/2}\le(2\norm w_3+\norm h_3)\cdot2(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{1/2}\norm h_3^{1/2}
\le4(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{3/2}\norm h_3^{1/2}$.  Finally, with
$\alpha=\norm{w'}_3$ and $\beta=\norm w_3$, both at most
$\norm w_3+\norm h_3$ and $|\alpha-\beta|\le\norm h_3$,
$|\mathcal Q(u+h)-\mathcal Q(u)|=\frac13|\alpha^3-\beta^3|
=\frac13|\alpha-\beta|(\alpha^2+\alpha\beta+\beta^2)
\le(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^2\norm h_3$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Fr\'echet derivative of $\mathcal Q$]
\label{prop:quotient-derivative}
$\mathcal Q$ is Fr\'echet differentiable at every $u\in L^3(\R^3;\R^3)$,
with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-derivative}
 D\mathcal Q(u)[h]=\langle A(u),h\rangle=\int_{\R^3}|w(u)|\,w(u)\cdot h\,dx
 \qquad(h\in L^3),
\end{equation}
and, with $w=w(u)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cp-derivative-remainder}
 \bigl|\mathcal Q(u+h)-\mathcal Q(u)-\langle A(u),h\rangle\bigr|
 \le6\,(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{3/2}\norm h_3^{3/2}
 \qquad(h\in L^3).
\end{equation}
In particular $D\mathcal Q(u)[g]=0$ for every $g\in\mathcal G_3$, and
$\norm{D\mathcal Q(u)}_{(L^3)^*}\le\norm w_3^2=(3\mathcal Q(u))^{2/3}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $w'=w(u+h)$, $A=A(u)$, $A'=A(u+h)$.  The functional
$h\mapsto\langle A,h\rangle$ is linear and bounded on $L^3$ with norm at
most $\norm A_{3/2}=\norm w_3^2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-frechet}), so
\eqref{eq:cp-derivative-remainder} implies Fr\'echet differentiability
with derivative \eqref{eq:cp-derivative}, the remainder being
$o(\norm h_3)$.

\emph{Upper bound.}  $w+h$ is admissible for $u+h$, so by
\eqref{eq:cp-F-taylor} with $v=w$,
\[
 \mathcal Q(u+h)\le F(w+h)
 \le\mathcal Q(u)+\langle A,h\rangle+(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2 .
\]

\emph{Lower bound.}  $w'-h$ is admissible for $u$, so by
\eqref{eq:cp-F-taylor} with $v=w'$ and increment $-h$,
\[
 \mathcal Q(u)\le F(w'-h)
 \le F(w')-\langle A',h\rangle+(\norm{w'}_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2 ,
\]
and $F(w')=\mathcal Q(u+h)$, $\norm{w'}_3\le\norm w_3+\norm h_3$ by
\eqref{eq:cp-contraction}.  Hence
\[
 \mathcal Q(u+h)\ge\mathcal Q(u)+\langle A,h\rangle+\langle A'-A,h\rangle
 -(\norm w_3+2\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2 .
\]

\emph{Remainder.}  By H\"older and \eqref{eq:cp-continuity},
$|\langle A'-A,h\rangle|\le\norm{A'-A}_{3/2}\norm h_3
\le4(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{3/2}\norm h_3^{3/2}$.  Combining the two
bounds,
\begin{align*}
 \bigl|\mathcal Q(u+h)-\mathcal Q(u)-\langle A,h\rangle\bigr|
 &\le4(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{3/2}\norm h_3^{3/2}
 +2(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)\norm h_3^2\\
 &\le6(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{3/2}\norm h_3^{3/2},
\end{align*}
using $\norm h_3^{1/2}\le(\norm w_3+\norm h_3)^{1/2}$ in the last term.
The final claims are Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer}(c) and
Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-frechet}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Unweighted regularity of the representative}
\label{subsec:quotient-divcurl}

The minimizing representative of Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer} was
constructed in $L^3$ alone, and no derivative of it has been used so far.
We now show that for a solenoidal datum of finite enstrophy the
representative in fact has a genuine unweighted distributional gradient in
$L^2(\R^3)$, with explicit constants and no smallness condition, and we
record the consequences of that regularity for the divergence defect and
for the strain work.

\emph{Notation for this subsection.}  For a real $3\times3$ matrix $M$ we
write $|M|$ for the operator norm, $|M|_F=(\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}^2)^{1/2}$ for
the Frobenius norm, and $\operatorname{sym}M=\frac12(M+M^{\mathsf T})$; by
the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality $|M|\le|M|_F$.  For a field
$z\in L^1_{\rm loc}(\R^3;\R^3)$ with distributional derivatives
$\partial_jz_i$, we write $\nabla z$ for the matrix field
$(\nabla z)_{ij}=\partial_jz_i$,
$\norm{\nabla z}_2^2=\sum_{i,j}\norm{\partial_jz_i}_2^2$,
$\operatorname{div}z=\partial_iz_i$ and
$(\operatorname{curl}z)_i=\epsilon_{ijk}\partial_jz_k$, with repeated
indices summed.  $C_S$ is the Sobolev constant of
Definition~\ref{def:sobolev-constant} and $C_{\mathbb P}$, $C_3$, $C_{3/2}$
are the operator norms of Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(b) and (F5).

\begin{lemma}[Trace control]\label{lem:trace-control}
Let $S$ be a real symmetric $3\times3$ matrix, let $e\in\R^3$ with $|e|=1$,
let $t\in[0,1]$, and put $d=\operatorname{tr}S$.  If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-constraint}
 d=-t\,e^{\mathsf T}Se ,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-trace}
 d^2\ \le\ \frac{2t^2}{t^2+2t+3}\,|S|_F^2\ \le\ \frac13\,|S|_F^2 .
\end{equation}
The function $t\mapsto2t^2/(t^2+2t+3)$ is strictly increasing on $[0,1]$
with value $\frac13$ at $t=1$, and for each $t\in(0,1]$ and each $d\in\R$
the left inequality in \eqref{eq:qdc-trace} is an equality for the matrix
which, in an orthonormal basis $(e,e',e'')$, has the diagonal form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-extremal}
 S=\operatorname{diag}\Bigl(-\frac dt,\ \frac d2\Bigl(1+\frac1t\Bigr),\
 \frac d2\Bigl(1+\frac1t\Bigr)\Bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $t=0$ then \eqref{eq:qdc-constraint} reads $d=0$ and both inequalities in
\eqref{eq:qdc-trace} are $0\le0\le\frac13|S|_F^2$.

Let $t\in(0,1]$.  Both $\operatorname{tr}S$ and $|S|_F$ are invariant under
$S\mapsto O^{\mathsf T}SO$ with $O$ orthogonal, and this replaces $e$ by
$O^{\mathsf T}e$, so we may rotate and assume $e=(1,0,0)$.  Then
$e^{\mathsf T}Se=S_{11}$, so \eqref{eq:qdc-constraint} gives $S_{11}=-d/t$
and $S_{22}+S_{33}=d-S_{11}=d+d/t$.  Discarding the six off-diagonal
squares and using $a^2+b^2\ge\frac12(a+b)^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-matrixchain}
 |S|_F^2\ \ge\ S_{11}^2+S_{22}^2+S_{33}^2
 \ \ge\ \frac{d^2}{t^2}+\frac12\Bigl(d+\frac dt\Bigr)^2
 =d^2\,\frac{2+(1+t)^2}{2t^2}
 =d^2\,\frac{t^2+2t+3}{2t^2},
\end{equation}
which is the left inequality in \eqref{eq:qdc-trace}.  For the right one,
$\frac{2t^2}{t^2+2t+3}\le\frac13$ is equivalent to $5t^2-2t-3\le0$, that is
to $(5t+3)(t-1)\le0$, which holds for $t\in[-\frac35,1]\supset[0,1]$, with
equality exactly at $t=1$.  The derivative of
$t\mapsto2t^2/(t^2+2t+3)$ has numerator
$4t(t^2+2t+3)-2t^2(2t+2)=4t^2+12t>0$ on $(0,1]$, so the function is
strictly increasing there, with value $\frac13$ at $t=1$; at $t=0$ it
vanishes.

For the extremal family, the matrix \eqref{eq:qdc-extremal} has
$\operatorname{tr}S=-\frac dt+d+\frac dt=d$ and $e^{\mathsf T}Se=-\frac dt$,
so $-t\,e^{\mathsf T}Se=d$ and \eqref{eq:qdc-constraint} holds; the two
quantities discarded in \eqref{eq:qdc-matrixchain}, namely
$\sum_{i\ne j}S_{ij}^2$ and $\frac12(S_{22}-S_{33})^2$, both vanish for it,
so both inequalities of \eqref{eq:qdc-matrixchain} are equalities and
$|S|_F^2=d^2(t^2+2t+3)/(2t^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:qdc-sharp}
Lemma~\ref{lem:trace-control} is sharp for every $t$, and the constant
$\frac13$ is the endpoint value at $t=1$.  This is the reason the estimates
below are uniform in the regularisation parameter $\delta$ introduced in the
proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:quotient-divcurl}: there $t=t_\delta<1$ for
every $\delta>0$, and the bound does not degenerate as $\delta\downarrow0$.
The constraint \eqref{eq:qdc-constraint} carries the whole content: for
$S=I$ and any $e$ one has $d^2=9$ and $\frac13|S|_F^2=1$, and
\eqref{eq:qdc-constraint} fails.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Unweighted div--curl estimate]
\label{prop:quotient-divcurl}
Let $u\in H^1(\R^3;\R^3)$ be solenoidal, and let $w=w(u)$ and $q=q(u)=w-u$
be the minimizing representative and the defect of
Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer}(b), which are defined because
$H^1(\R^3)\subset L^3(\R^3)$.  Then $w$ and $q$ have distributional
gradients in $L^2(\R^3)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-spaces}
 w,\,q\in L^6(\R^3;\R^3),\qquad
 \nabla w,\ \nabla q\in L^2(\R^3;\R^{3\times3}),
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad
 w,\,q\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\R^3;\R^3),
\end{equation}
with
\begin{align}
 \norm{\nabla w}_2^2&\le\tfrac54\,\norm{\nabla u}_2^2,
 \label{eq:qdc-w}\\
 \norm{\nabla q}_2^2=\norm{\operatorname{div}w}_2^2
 &\le\tfrac14\,\norm{\nabla u}_2^2 .
 \label{eq:qdc-q}
\end{align}
Moreover $\operatorname{curl}q=0$ and $\operatorname{curl}w=\operatorname{curl}u$
almost everywhere, $\operatorname{div}q=\operatorname{div}w$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-sobolev}
 \norm w_6\le C_S\norm{\nabla w}_2\le\bigl(\tfrac54\bigr)^{1/2}C_S\norm{\nabla u}_2,
 \qquad
 \norm q_6\le C_S\norm{\nabla q}_2 .
\end{equation}
No smallness of any norm of $u$ is assumed. These unweighted statements
alone do not assert $w\in L^2(\R^3)$. With the additional hypothesis
$|x|\nabla u\in L^2$, the review-pending Theorem~\ref{cm:snapshot}
establishes that membership.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 0: two elementary inequalities and the div--curl identities.}
For $f\in L^2\cap L^6(\R^3)$, H\"older's inequality with exponents
$\frac43$ and $4$ applied to $|f|^{3/2}\cdot|f|^{3/2}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-interp}
 \norm f_3\le\norm f_2^{1/2}\norm f_6^{1/2},
\end{equation}
componentwise and hence, after summing, for fields; in particular
$H^1(\R^3)\subset L^3(\R^3)$ once the Sobolev embedding below is available.
Next, if $f\in H^1(\R^3)$ is scalar and $f_n\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ with
$f_n\to f$ in $H^1$ (cutoff and mollification as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:gradient-closure}), then by \eqref{eq:sobolev-cc} the
sequence $(f_n)$ is Cauchy in $L^6$, its $L^6$ limit agrees a.e.\ with $f$
along a subsequence converging a.e., and $\norm f_6\le C_S\norm{\nabla f}_2$.
For a field $z$ with components in $H^1$, the triangle inequality in $L^3$
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-sobolev-field}
 \norm z_6^2=\norm{|z|^2}_3=\Bigl\|\sum_iz_i^2\Bigr\|_3
 \le\sum_i\norm{z_i^2}_3=\sum_i\norm{z_i}_6^2
 \le C_S^2\sum_i\norm{\nabla z_i}_2^2=C_S^2\norm{\nabla z}_2^2 .
\end{equation}

For $z\in H^1(\R^3;\R^3)$ we use the two identities
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-hodge}
 \norm{\nabla z}_2^2=\norm{\operatorname{curl}z}_2^2+\norm{\operatorname{div}z}_2^2,
 \qquad
 \norm{\operatorname{sym}\nabla z}_2^2
 =\tfrac12\norm{\operatorname{curl}z}_2^2+\norm{\operatorname{div}z}_2^2 .
\end{equation}
Both follow from (F4).  Indeed $\widehat{\partial_jz_i}=2\pi i\xi_j\hat z_i$,
so $\norm{\nabla z}_2^2=4\pi^2\int|\xi|^2|\hat z|^2d\xi$,
$\norm{\operatorname{div}z}_2^2=4\pi^2\int|\xi\cdot\hat z|^2d\xi$ and
$\norm{\operatorname{curl}z}_2^2=4\pi^2\int|\xi\times\hat z|^2d\xi$;
writing $\hat z=a+ib$ with $a,b$ real and applying Lagrange's identity
$|\xi\times a|^2+(\xi\cdot a)^2=|\xi|^2|a|^2$ to $a$ and to $b$ gives
$|\xi\times\hat z|^2+|\xi\cdot\hat z|^2=|\xi|^2|\hat z|^2$, which is the
left identity.  For the right one, expanding the symmetric part,
\[
 |\operatorname{sym}\nabla z|_F^2
 =\tfrac14\sum_{i,j}(\partial_jz_i+\partial_iz_j)^2
 =\tfrac12\sum_{i,j}(\partial_jz_i)^2
 +\tfrac12\sum_{i,j}\partial_jz_i\,\partial_iz_j ,
\]
and by (F4) again
$\int\sum_{i,j}\partial_jz_i\,\partial_iz_j\,dx
=4\pi^2\int\sum_{i,j}\xi_j\hat z_i\,\overline{\xi_i\hat z_j}\,d\xi
=4\pi^2\int|\xi\cdot\hat z|^2d\xi=\norm{\operatorname{div}z}_2^2$;
combining with the left identity gives the right one.

\emph{Step 1: a nondegenerate problem in $L^2\cap L^3$.}  Assume to begin with that
$u\in H^m(\R^3;\R^3)$ for some integer $m\ge4$ and that $u$ is solenoidal;
the general case is Step~6.  Then $u\in L^2$, and $\nabla u\in H^3$, so by
Lemma~\ref{lem:qe-embedding} and \eqref{eq:qdc-interp} we have
$u\in L^2\cap L^3$ and $\nabla u\in L^2\cap L^3$.

Put
\[
 X=L^2(\R^3;\R^3)\cap L^3(\R^3;\R^3),\qquad
 \norm z_X=\norm z_2+\norm z_3,
 \qquad
 G_X=\overline{\{\nabla\phi:\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)\}}^{\,X}.
\]
The map $z\mapsto(z,z)$ is an isometry of $X$ onto a subspace of
$L^2\times L^3$ (with the sum norm) which is closed: if $(z_n,z_n)\to(f,g)$
then a subsequence converges a.e.\ to $f$ and to $g$, so $f=g$ a.e.  Since
$L^2\times L^3$ is reflexive by (F1), so is $X$.  The set $u+G_X$ is a
nonempty closed affine subspace of $X$, hence weakly closed by (F2).  It is
essential that the closure defining $G_X$ is taken in $X$; no $L^2$ property
of the unregularised minimizer is used or asserted.

For $\delta>0$ set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-regularizer}
 r_\delta(z)=\sqrt{\delta^2+|z|^2},\qquad
 f_\delta(z)=\frac{(\delta^2+|z|^2)^{3/2}-\delta^3}{3},\qquad
 j_\delta(z)=r_\delta(z)\,z\qquad(z\in\R^3).
\end{equation}
The function $f_\delta$ depends on $z$ only through $\rho=|z|$, and its
derivative in $\rho$ is $\rho\sqrt{\delta^2+\rho^2}$, so that
$f_\delta(z)=\int_0^{|z|}s\sqrt{\delta^2+s^2}\,ds$ and
$\nabla f_\delta=j_\delta$.  From $s\le\sqrt{\delta^2+s^2}\le\delta+s$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-growth}
 \frac{|z|^3}3\le f_\delta(z),
 \qquad
 \frac{\delta|z|^2}2\le f_\delta(z),
 \qquad
 f_\delta(z)\le\frac{|z|^3}3+\frac{\delta|z|^2}2 .
\end{equation}
Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-Dj}
 Dj_\delta(z)=r_\delta(z)\,I+\frac{z\otimes z}{r_\delta(z)},
\end{equation}
a positive definite symmetric matrix whose eigenvalues are $r_\delta(z)$
(twice) and $(\delta^2+2|z|^2)/r_\delta(z)$, all lying in
$[r_\delta(z),2r_\delta(z)]$; in particular $f_\delta$ is strictly convex on
all of $\R^3$, including at $z=0$.

Let $F_\delta(z)=\int_{\R^3}f_\delta(z)\,dx$ and minimise $F_\delta$ over
$u+G_X$.  By \eqref{eq:qdc-growth} a minimising sequence is bounded in
$X$; $F_\delta$ is convex and, by Fatou's lemma along an a.e.\ convergent
subsequence, strongly lower semicontinuous, hence weakly lower
semicontinuous by (F2); and $u+G_X$ is weakly closed.  By (F1) a minimiser
$w_\delta=u+q_\delta$, $q_\delta\in G_X$, exists, and strict convexity of
$f_\delta$ makes it unique.

Since $|j_\delta(z)|=r_\delta(z)|z|\le\delta|z|+|z|^2$, we have
$j_\delta(w_\delta)\in L^2+L^{3/2}$, and for $g\in G_X$ and $|s|\le1$ the
integrand of $F_\delta(w_\delta+sg)$ is differentiable in $s$ with
$|j_\delta(w_\delta+sg)\cdot g|\le(\delta|w_\delta|+\delta|g|+(|w_\delta|+|g|)^2)|g|$,
an integrable dominant by H\"older's inequality.  Differentiating at $s=0$
gives the Euler--Lagrange condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-EL}
 \int_{\R^3}j_\delta(w_\delta)\cdot g\,dx=0\qquad(g\in G_X).
\end{equation}
Taking $g=\nabla\phi$, $\phi\in C_c^\infty$, gives
$\operatorname{div}j_\delta(w_\delta)=0$ in the sense of distributions.
Also $q_\delta$ is an $X$-limit, hence an $L^3$-limit, of gradients of
$C_c^\infty$ functions, so $\operatorname{curl}q_\delta=0$ and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-curl}
 \operatorname{curl}w_\delta=\operatorname{curl}u
 \qquad\text{in the sense of distributions}.
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 2: fixed-$\delta$ weak derivatives, proved before they are used.}
We begin by recording the weighted monotonicity of $j_\delta$.  For
$a,b\in\R^3$ put $B_\delta(a,b)=\delta+|a|+|b|$ and $z(\theta)=b+\theta(a-b)$.
Integrating \eqref{eq:qdc-Dj} along $z(\theta)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-mono}
 \bigl(j_\delta(a)-j_\delta(b)\bigr)\cdot(a-b)
 \ \ge\ \frac1{32}\,B_\delta(a,b)\,|a-b|^2,
 \qquad
 \bigl|j_\delta(a)-j_\delta(b)\bigr|\le2\,B_\delta(a,b)\,|a-b| .
\end{equation}
For the second bound use $|Dj_\delta(z(\theta))|\le2r_\delta(z(\theta))
\le2(\delta+\max(|a|,|b|))\le2B_\delta(a,b)$.  For the lower bound, put
$M=\max(|a|,|b|)$ and note $|a-b|\le2M$.  If $M=|a|$ then for
$\frac34\le\theta\le1$ one has
$|z(\theta)|\ge|a|-(1-\theta)|a-b|\ge M-\frac14\cdot2M=\frac M2$, so
$\int_0^1r_\delta(z(\theta))\,d\theta\ge\frac14\cdot\frac M2=\frac M8
\ge\frac{|a|+|b|}{16}$; if $M=|b|$ the same estimate holds using
$0\le\theta\le\frac14$.  Since also
$\int_0^1r_\delta(z(\theta))\,d\theta\ge\delta$, taking half the sum of the
two lower bounds gives
$\int_0^1r_\delta(z(\theta))\,d\theta\ge\frac1{32}B_\delta(a,b)$, and
\eqref{eq:qdc-Dj} bounds the quadratic form of $Dj_\delta$ from below by
$r_\delta$ times the identity.

Fix $k\in\{1,2,3\}$, $h\ne0$, and write $\tau_hf=f(\cdot+he_k)$.  Translation
is an isometry of $X$ mapping $\{\nabla\phi:\phi\in C_c^\infty\}$ onto
itself, so $G_X$ is translation invariant.  Replacing $g$ by $\tau_{-h}g$ in
\eqref{eq:qdc-EL} and changing variables gives
$\int j_\delta(\tau_hw_\delta)\cdot g\,dx=0$ for every $g\in G_X$.
Subtracting the two identities and testing with
$g=\tau_hq_\delta-q_\delta\in G_X$, and inserting
$\tau_hq_\delta-q_\delta=(\tau_hw_\delta-w_\delta)-(\tau_hu-u)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-difftest}
 \int\bigl(j_\delta(\tau_hw_\delta)-j_\delta(w_\delta)\bigr)\cdot
 (\tau_hw_\delta-w_\delta)\,dx
 =\int\bigl(j_\delta(\tau_hw_\delta)-j_\delta(w_\delta)\bigr)\cdot
 (\tau_hu-u)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
all four integrals being absolutely convergent because
$w_\delta,u\in X$ and $|j_\delta(z)|\le\delta|z|+|z|^2$.  Put
$B_h=\delta+|\tau_hw_\delta|+|w_\delta|$ and
$W_h=\int B_h|\tau_hw_\delta-w_\delta|^2dx$.  By \eqref{eq:qdc-mono} and the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality with weight $B_h$, \eqref{eq:qdc-difftest} gives
\[
 \tfrac1{32}W_h\le2\,W_h^{1/2}
 \Bigl(\int B_h|\tau_hu-u|^2dx\Bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
If $W_h=0$ the conclusion below is trivial; otherwise divide by $W_h^{1/2}$
and square, so that $W_h\le4096\int B_h|\tau_hu-u|^2dx$.  By H\"older's
inequality with exponents $3$ and $\frac32$ and
$\norm{\tau_hw_\delta}_3=\norm{w_\delta}_3$,
\[
 \int B_h|\tau_hu-u|^2dx
 \le\delta\norm{\tau_hu-u}_2^2+2\norm{w_\delta}_3\norm{\tau_hu-u}_3^2 .
\]
Since $B_h\ge\delta$ and $\norm{\tau_hf-f}_p\le|h|\,\norm{\partial_kf}_p$
for $f$ with $\partial_kf\in L^p$, we obtain, for each \emph{fixed} $\delta>0$
and all $h\ne0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-diffquot}
 \Bigl\|\frac{\tau_hw_\delta-w_\delta}h\Bigr\|_2^2
 \le4096\Bigl(\norm{\partial_ku}_2^2
 +2\delta^{-1}\norm{w_\delta}_3\norm{\partial_ku}_3^2\Bigr)<\infty,
\end{equation}
the right side being finite exactly because $u\in H^m$ supplies
$\partial_ku\in L^2\cap L^3$.  This bound is not uniform in $\delta$, and it
is not used as if it were.

The passage from \eqref{eq:qdc-diffquot} to a weak derivative needs no
elliptic regularity theorem.  Let $h_n\to0$; by (F1) a subsequence of the
difference quotients converges weakly in $L^2$ to some $v_k$.  For
$\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$,
\[
 \int\frac{\tau_hw_\delta-w_\delta}{h}\,\phi\,dx
 =\int w_\delta\,\frac{\tau_{-h}\phi-\phi}h\,dx
 \longrightarrow-\int w_\delta\,\partial_k\phi\,dx ,
\]
because $(\tau_{-h}\phi-\phi)/h\to-\partial_k\phi$ uniformly with supports
in a fixed compact set while $w_\delta\in L^2$.  Hence
$\int v_k\phi=-\int w_\delta\partial_k\phi$, that is $v_k=\partial_kw_\delta$
distributionally, with the bound \eqref{eq:qdc-diffquot}.  Doing this for
$k=1,2,3$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-H1}
 w_\delta\in H^1(\R^3;\R^3)\subset L^6(\R^3;\R^3)\qquad(\delta>0).
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 3: a chain rule, and the pointwise constraint.}  We use the
following form of the chain rule.  Let $N,M\ge1$, let
$v\in L^6(\R^3;\R^N)$ have distributional gradient $\nabla v\in L^2$, and let
$\Psi\in C^1(\R^N;\R^M)$ satisfy $\Psi(0)=0$ and $|D\Psi(z)|\le a+b|z|$ for
constants $a,b\ge0$.  Then $\Psi(v)\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}(\R^3;\R^M)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-chainrule}
 \partial_i\Psi(v)=D\Psi(v)\,\partial_iv\quad\text{a.e.}\qquad(i=1,2,3).
\end{equation}
Indeed, put $v_n=\rho_{1/n}*v$.  By (F3), $v_n\in C^\infty$, $v_n\to v$ in
$L^6$ and $\nabla v_n=\rho_{1/n}*\nabla v\to\nabla v$ in $L^2$; passing to a
subsequence \cite[Thm.~4.9, p.~94]{Brezis2011} gives $v_n\to v$ and
$\nabla v_n\to\nabla v$ a.e.\ together with $|v_n|\le g\in L^6$ and
$|\nabla v_n|\le G\in L^2$.  For smooth $v_n$ the identity
\eqref{eq:qdc-chainrule} is classical.  On a compact $K$ we have
$|D\Psi(v_n)\nabla v_n|\le(a+bg)G$, which lies in $L^1(K)$ because
$aG\in L^2$ and $bgG\in L^{3/2}$ by H\"older with $\frac16+\frac12=\frac23$;
and $|\Psi(v_n)|\le(a+b|v_n|)|v_n|\le ag+bg^2\in L^1(K)$ because $g\in L^6$.
Dominated convergence therefore lets us pass to the limit in
$\int\Psi(v_n)\,\partial_i\phi\,dx=-\int\partial_i\Psi(v_n)\,\phi\,dx$ for
$\phi\in C_c^\infty$, which is \eqref{eq:qdc-chainrule}.

Apply \eqref{eq:qdc-chainrule} with $\Psi=j_\delta$, which satisfies
$j_\delta(0)=0$ and $|Dj_\delta(z)|\le2r_\delta(z)\le2\delta+2|z|$, and with
$v=w_\delta$, admissible by \eqref{eq:qdc-H1}.  Since
$\partial_ir_\delta(w_\delta)=w_{\delta,j}\partial_iw_{\delta,j}/r_\delta(w_\delta)$,
the distribution $\operatorname{div}j_\delta(w_\delta)$ is the locally
integrable function
\[
 r_\delta(w_\delta)\operatorname{div}w_\delta
 +\frac{w_{\delta,i}w_{\delta,j}}{r_\delta(w_\delta)}\,\partial_iw_{\delta,j},
\]
and by Step~1 it vanishes as a distribution, hence, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:density}(a), almost everywhere:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-ae}
 r_\delta(w_\delta)\,\operatorname{div}w_\delta
 +\frac{w_{\delta,i}w_{\delta,j}}{r_\delta(w_\delta)}\,\partial_iw_{\delta,j}=0
 \qquad\text{a.e.\ on }\R^3 .
\end{equation}
Write $S_\delta=\operatorname{sym}\nabla w_\delta$ and
$d_\delta=\operatorname{tr}S_\delta=\operatorname{div}w_\delta$.  Since
$w_\delta\otimes w_\delta$ is symmetric,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-symmetrize}
 w_{\delta,i}w_{\delta,j}\,\partial_iw_{\delta,j}
 =w_{\delta,i}w_{\delta,j}\,(S_\delta)_{ij},
\end{equation}
so \eqref{eq:qdc-ae} involves only the symmetric part of $\nabla w_\delta$.
Put
\[
 t_\delta=\frac{|w_\delta|^2}{\delta^2+|w_\delta|^2}\in[0,1),
 \qquad
 e_\delta=\begin{cases}w_\delta/|w_\delta|,&w_\delta\ne0,\\
 (1,0,0),&w_\delta=0,\end{cases}
\]
a measurable unit field.  At a point where $w_\delta\ne0$ and
\eqref{eq:qdc-ae} holds, dividing \eqref{eq:qdc-ae} by
$r_\delta(w_\delta)>0$ and using \eqref{eq:qdc-symmetrize} gives
$d_\delta+t_\delta\,e_\delta^{\mathsf T}S_\delta e_\delta=0$.  At a point
where $w_\delta=0$ and \eqref{eq:qdc-ae} holds, the second term of
\eqref{eq:qdc-ae} vanishes identically and $r_\delta(0)=\delta$, so
\eqref{eq:qdc-ae} reads $\delta\,\operatorname{div}w_\delta=0$ with
$\delta>0$; hence $d_\delta=0$ there, and since $t_\delta=0$ as well the
same relation holds with the arbitrary unit vector $e_\delta$.  This is the
only place at which the regularisation is indispensable: at a zero of the
unregularised field the corresponding equation degenerates to $0=0$ and
supplies no constraint.  Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-pointwise}
 d_\delta=-t_\delta\,e_\delta^{\mathsf T}S_\delta e_\delta,
 \qquad t_\delta\in[0,1],
 \qquad\text{a.e.\ on }\R^3 .
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 4: uniform bounds.}  By \eqref{eq:qdc-pointwise} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:trace-control}, $d_\delta^2\le\frac13|S_\delta|_F^2$ almost
everywhere.  Integrating and using the second identity of
\eqref{eq:qdc-hodge} for $w_\delta\in H^1$, and writing
$C=\norm{\operatorname{curl}w_\delta}_2^2$ and
$D_\delta=\norm{\operatorname{div}w_\delta}_2^2$,
\[
 D_\delta\le\tfrac13\norm{S_\delta}_2^2
 =\tfrac13\Bigl(\tfrac C2+D_\delta\Bigr),
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad
 \tfrac23D_\delta\le\tfrac16C,
 \qquad D_\delta\le\tfrac C4 .
\]
By \eqref{eq:qdc-curl}, and since $w_\delta,u\in H^1$ so that both curls are
$L^2$ functions, $C=\norm{\operatorname{curl}u}_2^2$; and since $u$ is
solenoidal the left identity of \eqref{eq:qdc-hodge} gives
$C=\norm{\nabla u}_2^2$.  Using the left identity of \eqref{eq:qdc-hodge}
for $w_\delta$ once more,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-uniform}
 \norm{\operatorname{div}w_\delta}_2^2\le\tfrac14\norm{\nabla u}_2^2,
 \qquad
 \norm{\nabla w_\delta}_2^2=C+D_\delta\le\tfrac54\norm{\nabla u}_2^2,
 \qquad
 \norm{w_\delta}_6\le\bigl(\tfrac54\bigr)^{1/2}C_S\norm{\nabla u}_2 ,
\end{equation}
the last by \eqref{eq:qdc-sobolev-field}.  Finally $q_\delta=w_\delta-u\in H^1$
is curl free, so by \eqref{eq:qdc-hodge}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-uniform-q}
 \norm{\nabla q_\delta}_2^2=\norm{\operatorname{div}q_\delta}_2^2
 =\norm{\operatorname{div}w_\delta}_2^2\le\tfrac14\norm{\nabla u}_2^2 .
\end{equation}
Unlike \eqref{eq:qdc-diffquot}, the bounds \eqref{eq:qdc-uniform} and
\eqref{eq:qdc-uniform-q} do not involve $\delta$.

\emph{Step 5: passage to the limit $\delta\downarrow0$.}  By minimality and
\eqref{eq:qdc-growth},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-energybound}
 F(w_\delta)\le F_\delta(w_\delta)\le F_\delta(u)
 \le F(u)+\tfrac\delta2\norm u_2^2 ,
\end{equation}
so $\norm{w_\delta}_3$ and hence $\norm{q_\delta}_3\le\norm{w_\delta}_3+\norm u_3$
are bounded for $0<\delta\le1$.  Let $\delta_n\downarrow0$.  By (F1) and
\eqref{eq:qdc-uniform} we may pass to a subsequence, not relabelled, along
which
\[
 q_{\delta_n}\rightharpoonup\bar q\ \text{in }L^3,\quad
 w_{\delta_n}\rightharpoonup\tilde w\ \text{in }L^6,\quad
 \nabla w_{\delta_n}\rightharpoonup V\ \text{in }L^2,\quad
 \operatorname{div}w_{\delta_n}\rightharpoonup\varsigma\ \text{in }L^2 .
\]
Since $G_X\subset\mathcal G_3$ and $\mathcal G_3$ is weakly closed by (F2),
$\bar q\in\mathcal G_3$, so $\bar w:=u+\bar q$ is admissible for
$\mathcal Q(u)$ and $F(\bar w)\ge\mathcal Q(u)$.

For the reverse inequality, let $w=w(u)$ and choose $\phi_m\in C_c^\infty$
with $g_m=\nabla\phi_m\to q(u)=w-u$ in $L^3$.  Each $u+g_m$ lies in $X$ and
is admissible for \emph{every} regularised problem, so by
\eqref{eq:qdc-growth}
\[
 F(w_\delta)\le F_\delta(w_\delta)\le F_\delta(u+g_m)
 \le F(u+g_m)+\tfrac\delta2\norm{u+g_m}_2^2 .
\]
Letting $\delta\downarrow0$ at \emph{fixed} $m$ gives
$\limsup_{\delta\downarrow0}F(w_\delta)\le F(u+g_m)$; the $L^2$ norm of
$u+g_m$, which is not controlled as $m\to\infty$, never has to be, because
the two limits are taken in this order.  Letting $m\to\infty$ and using the
continuity of $F$ on $L^3$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:cubic-frechet}) gives
\[
 \limsup_{\delta\downarrow0}F(w_\delta)\le F(w)=\mathcal Q(u).
\]
On the other hand $w_{\delta_n}=u+q_{\delta_n}\rightharpoonup\bar w$ in
$L^3$, so $F(\bar w)\le\liminf_nF(w_{\delta_n})$ by (F2).  Hence
$F(\bar w)=\mathcal Q(u)$, and by the uniqueness in
Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer}(b), $\bar w=w$.  Consequently
$w_{\delta_n}\rightharpoonup w$ in $L^3$.  No uniform convexity of $L^3$ and
no strong convergence of $w_{\delta_n}$ is needed for what follows.

We identify the remaining weak limits.  For $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\R^3)$ one
has $\phi\in L^{3/2}\cap L^{6/5}$, so
$\int\tilde w\phi=\lim\int w_{\delta_n}\phi=\int w\phi$ and hence
$\tilde w=w$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:density}(a); thus $w\in L^6$ with
$\norm w_6\le\liminf\norm{w_{\delta_n}}_6$.  Similarly
\[
 \int V_{ik}\phi\,dx=\lim_n\int\partial_kw_{\delta_n,i}\,\phi\,dx
 =-\lim_n\int w_{\delta_n,i}\,\partial_k\phi\,dx
 =-\int w_i\,\partial_k\phi\,dx ,
\]
so $V=\nabla w$ distributionally, and likewise
$\varsigma=\operatorname{div}w$.  Lower semicontinuity (F2) together with
\eqref{eq:qdc-uniform} and \eqref{eq:qdc-uniform-q} now gives
\eqref{eq:qdc-w} and the inequality in \eqref{eq:qdc-q}, as well as
$\norm{\nabla q}_2^2\le\frac14\norm{\nabla u}_2^2$, where
$q=w-u$ has $\nabla q=\nabla w-\nabla u\in L^2$ and $q\in L^6$ because
$u\in H^1\subset L^6$.  Since $L^6\subset L^2_{\rm loc}$, this proves
\eqref{eq:qdc-spaces}.  Also $q=q(u)\in\mathcal G_3$ is an $L^3$ limit of
gradients of $C_c^\infty$ functions, so $\operatorname{curl}q=0$ as a
distribution and hence a.e., $\operatorname{curl}w=\operatorname{curl}u$ a.e.,
and $\operatorname{div}q=\operatorname{div}w$ because $u$ is solenoidal.

It remains to upgrade the inequality in \eqref{eq:qdc-q} to the stated
equality, and to prove \eqref{eq:qdc-sobolev}.  Both follow from one cutoff.
Let $z\in L^6(\R^3;\R^3)$ with $\nabla z\in L^2$.  Then $\chi_Rz\in H^1$,
\[
 \nabla(\chi_Rz)=\chi_R\nabla z+z\otimes\nabla\chi_R,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{div}(\chi_Rz)=\chi_R\operatorname{div}z+z\cdot\nabla\chi_R,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{curl}(\chi_Rz)=\chi_R\operatorname{curl}z+\nabla\chi_R\times z,
\]
and, by H\"older's inequality with $\frac12=\frac13+\frac16$, each error
term is bounded in $L^2$ by
\[
 \sqrt3\,\norm{\nabla\chi_R}_3\,\norm z_{L^6(\{R\le|x|\le2R\})}
 =\sqrt3\,\norm{\nabla\chi}_3\,\norm z_{L^6(\{R\le|x|\le2R\})},
\]
which tends to $0$ as $R\to\infty$ by dominated convergence, since
$\norm{\nabla\chi_R}_3$ does not depend on $R$.  The main terms converge in $L^2$ to $\nabla z$,
$\operatorname{div}z$, $\operatorname{curl}z$.  Applying
\eqref{eq:qdc-sobolev-field} to $\chi_Rz\in H^1$ and letting $R\to\infty$,
with $\norm{\chi_Rz}_6\to\norm z_6$ by dominated convergence, gives
$\norm z_6\le C_S\norm{\nabla z}_2$; applying \eqref{eq:qdc-hodge} to
$\chi_Rz$ and letting $R\to\infty$ gives \eqref{eq:qdc-hodge} for $z$ itself.
With $z=w$ and $z=q$ this yields \eqref{eq:qdc-sobolev}, and with $z=q$,
using $\operatorname{curl}q=0$ and $\operatorname{div}q=\operatorname{div}w$,
it yields $\norm{\nabla q}_2^2=\norm{\operatorname{div}w}_2^2$, completing
\eqref{eq:qdc-q}.  This proves the proposition for solenoidal $u\in H^m$,
$m\ge4$.

\emph{Step 6: from high Sobolev data to every solenoidal $H^1$ datum.}  Let
$u\in H^1(\R^3;\R^3)$ be solenoidal and put $u_n=G_{1/n}u$ with $G_s$ the
heat semigroup of Definition~\ref{def:quotient}(d).  Then
$\hat u_n=e^{-4\pi^2|\xi|^2/n}\hat u$, so $u_n\in H^m$ for every $m$;
$\xi\cdot\hat u_n=0$ a.e.\ because $\xi\cdot\hat u=0$ a.e., so $u_n$ is
solenoidal by the Fourier characterisation of solenoidality established in
the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:leray}(a); and $u_n\to u$ in $H^1$ by dominated
convergence in $\xi$.  By \eqref{eq:qdc-sobolev-field} and
\eqref{eq:qdc-interp}, $u_n\to u$ in $L^6$ and in $L^3$.  By
Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-stability}, estimate \eqref{eq:cp-strong},
$w_n:=w(u_n)\to w:=w(u)$ in $L^3$, and $q_n:=q(u_n)\to q:=q(u)$ in $L^3$.

Steps~1--5 apply to each $u_n$, giving $w_n\in L^6$, $\nabla w_n\in L^2$ and
\[
 \norm{\nabla w_n}_2^2\le\tfrac54\norm{\nabla u_n}_2^2,
 \qquad
 \norm{\operatorname{div}w_n}_2^2\le\tfrac14\norm{\nabla u_n}_2^2,
 \qquad
 \norm{w_n}_6\le\bigl(\tfrac54\bigr)^{1/2}C_S\norm{\nabla u_n}_2 .
\]
Since $\norm{\nabla u_n}_2\to\norm{\nabla u}_2$, these sequences are bounded,
and exactly as in Step~5 we may extract weak limits in $L^6$ and $L^2$ and
identify them, this time using the strong (hence weak) $L^3$ convergence
$w_n\to w$, as $w$, $\nabla w$ and $\operatorname{div}w$.  Lower
semicontinuity (F2) gives \eqref{eq:qdc-w} and the inequality of
\eqref{eq:qdc-q}; the statements about $q$, the curl, and the cutoff
identities are obtained verbatim as at the end of Step~5.  This proves the
proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The order of the argument]\label{rem:qdc-order}
The order of Steps~1--5 is essential and is not an accident of exposition.
The problem is solved in $X$ at the outset using only convexity and coercivity, with
no derivative of $w_\delta$ in play; the membership $w_\delta\in H^1$ is then
proved by translation differences from the Euler--Lagrange identity alone;
only afterwards is that equation divided by $r_\delta$ and combined with
Lemma~\ref{lem:trace-control}; and only at the very end is $\delta$ sent to
zero.  Dividing the unregularised stationarity condition on the zero set of
$w$, or assuming $\nabla w\in L^2$ in order to derive a bound for it, would
not justify \eqref{eq:qdc-w}--\eqref{eq:qdc-q}.  The numbers $\frac54$ and
$\frac14$ are the constants produced by the displayed algebra; no claim of
optimality is made for them.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[The divergence defect]\label{cor:quotient-defect}
Let $u\in H^1(\R^3;\R^3)$ be solenoidal, and let $w=w(u)$, $q=q(u)$ and
$A=A(u)=|w|w$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-minimizer}(b).  Then
$A\in W^{1,3/2}(\R^3;\R^3)$ with $|\nabla A|_F\le2|w|\,|\nabla w|_F$ a.e.,
and the function
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-sigma}
 \sigma=\begin{cases}
 \dfrac{w_iw_j\,\partial_iw_j}{|w|^2}
 =\dfrac{w}{|w|}\cdot\nabla|w|, & w\ne0,\\[2mm]
 0,& w=0,
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-sigmaid}
 \begin{gathered}
 \sigma=-\operatorname{div}w
 \quad\text{a.e.\ and in the sense of distributions,}\\
 \text{including across }\{w=0\},
 \qquad
 \norm\sigma_2\le\tfrac12\norm{\nabla u}_2 .
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Moreover $q$ is the gradient of the Newtonian potential of $\sigma$, in the
sense that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qdc-potential}
 \partial_kq_j=R_jR_k\sigma\qquad(j,k=1,2,3),
\end{equation}
with $R_j$ the Riesz transforms of Definition~\ref{def:lp}; equivalently,
$q$ is the unique field in $L^6(\R^3;\R^3)$ with $\nabla q\in L^2$,
$\operatorname{curl}q=0$ and $\operatorname{div}q=-\sigma$.  Formula
\eqref{eq:qdc-potential} is not a claim that an unregularised Newton
convolution of $\sigma$ converges absolutely pointwise.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: a level-set fact.}  Let $f\in L^6(\R^3)$ be real valued with
$\nabla f\in L^2$.  Then
